# Millet Genealogy — Research Notes

Compiled from the sources cited under each person. Every fact below is tied to a record or publication; where a link between people is inferred rather than documented it is marked **probable** and the reasoning is given. The interactive tree (`index.html`) is built from the same data. Phase 1 (August–September 2026) built the five lines; Phase 2 (September 6–7, 2026) proved or tested the weak links, took the Moyer line back three generations, and filled in the direct-line women. A log of Phase 2 is at the end.


## The questions this research set out to answer

1. **Was an ancestor involved in "cracking" the Liberty Bell?** The lore is half right. John Jacob Mickley Jr. (1737–1808) of Egypt, Whitehall Township, hauled the State House bell out of Philadelphia in September 1777 so the British could not melt it into cannon. His wagon broke down under the bell's weight at Bethlehem on September 23 (the Moravian diary records it), and a neighbor, Frederick Leaser, finished the haul to Zion Reformed Church in Allentown, where the bell was hidden under the floor until June 1778. Nothing in any account connects the Mickleys with the crack, which dates from the 1840s. Mickley's eleven-year-old son rode on the wagon.

2. **Are Michelet, Mickley, and Millet the same family?** Michelet and Mickley are: Jean Jacques Michelet, born at Zweibrücken in 1697 to a Huguenot family from Metz, landed at Philadelphia in 1733 and his name became Mickley in Pennsylvania. Millet is a different family that married into the Mickley line: Catharine Moyer, granddaughter of Johann Peter Mickley through Susanna Mickley Moyer, married Frederick Millet of Orwigsburg about 1862. The Millets were Millot, Mylot or Miloth in nearly every record before the 1840s — **but not quite every one**: the very earliest record of the family in Pennsylvania, a Land Office warrant application of 27 July 1767, spells him **Frederick Millet** in the clerk's own hand, and the printed *Pennsylvania Archives* does the same on Capt. Strouch's pay roll of 1780. The resemblance to the French painter's surname is still, as far as the records show, a coincidence, but it no longer rests on the spelling argument (see below).

3. **The Millet line above Frederick (1820–1864).** Reconstructed from deeds, tax lists, militia rolls, and newspapers rather than from any single genealogy. See the Millet / Millot section. Phase 2 searched FamilySearch's full-text index of Berks and Schuylkill deeds, probate, and court dockets and found nothing new; the links from Sgt. Frederick to his supposed children and from Martin to Frederick remain probable, and the records that would decide them are named under Open leads.

4. **Christine Nickerson's side.** Traced from Bayonne back through Coeymans, New York, and Ridgefield, Connecticut, to William Nickerson (1604–1690), founder of Chatham on Cape Cod, and now one generation further to Anne Busby's parents, Nicholas and Bridget Busby, who crossed with the family in 1637; and on the Christoffersen side to Denmark and to Egersund, Norway, where Olava Olsdatter's parentage is now proved from the parish registers and censuses.

5. **The Moyer line.** Thomas Moyer (1804–1869), husband of Susanna Mickley, is now tied by his Egypt Reformed baptism and a Lehigh County Orphans' Court guardianship of 1818 to Jacob Meyer (1781–bef. 1818) and Christina Newan of South Whitehall, and through the FamilySearch tree to Peter Meyer Sr. (c. 1752–1838) and Catharina Kern, with Martin Meyer Sr. (1730–1807) above them marked probable.

6. **The women of the direct line.** Four great-grandmothers and grandmothers who had been names with dates now have parents and records: Mary Catherine Martin (1891–1971) of Grier City, daughter of Adolph Martin, a German immigrant of 1881, and Catharine Herring; Marie Violet Williams (1922–2004), daughter of William Earl Williams of Gilberton, with Evelyn Beck identified as her stepmother rather than her mother; Julia Baran (b. 1907), daughter of George and Mary Baran, Slovak immigrants in Bayonne; and Louise Sophie "Lulu" Mihm (1890–1977), daughter of Joseph Mihm and Louisa Mayer of Jersey City.

## How each line is established

**Michelet of Metz.** The 1893 *Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America* prints a family table of the Michelets of Metz sent from Berlin in 1883 by Prof. Karl Ludwig Michelet, a descendant of the emigrant's younger brother. The surviving Reformed registers of Metz (FamilySearch, "France, Protestant Church Records, 1536–1902") confirm that table generation by generation: Jacquemin's marriage to Suzanne Wiriot (1591), Daniel's baptism (1592) and marriage to Marie Colin (1615), Jacques's marriage to Anne Philpin (1646) and burial (1685), and Louis's baptism (17 December 1675, parents "Jacq. Michelet" and "A. Philpin"). Two corrections to what circulates online came out of this: Jacques's wife was Anne Philpin, not "Anne Du Bois" (the Berlin table lists an Anne "veuve Dubois" in his household, which is where the name came from), and the emigrant was born at Zweibrücken because his Protestant parents could not marry in French Metz after 1685. The Zweibrücken registers themselves are not indexed online; the Berlin cousin already warned in 1883 that they were probably destroyed under Louis XIV. The Michelets of Berlin (silk manufacturers, then the philosopher Karl Ludwig Michelet) and the Norwegian Michelets (army officers descended from two brothers who left Metz in 1644) are collateral branches.

**Mickley of Lehigh County.** The 1893 genealogy, the Wikipedia articles on the settler and the soldier, the Zion Reformed Church (Allentown) account of the bell, Find a Grave, and FamilySearch tree profiles with attached records. Peter Mickley's eleven children and Susanna Mickley Moyer's move to Tamaqua come from the genealogy and from the Egypt Reformed Church baptisms.

**Moyer.** Thomas Moyer was born 19 November 1804 in Whitehall Township and baptized at Egypt Reformed Church on 26 January 1805 as Thomas Mayer, son of Jacob Mayer and Christina (indexed baptism). The link to that family is the Lehigh County Orphans' Court docket for February term 1818 (p. 207, no. 14, read from the page image): Christina Moyer, widow of Jacob Moyer of South Whitehall Township, yeoman, petitioned for guardians for her minor children, and Peter Moyer was appointed for Thomas and Stephen. Thomas married Susanna Mickley in South Whitehall in 1833; the 1850 and 1860 Tamaqua censuses (household 80 in the east ward; household 944, page 125) give the nine children, his trade as a potter in 1850, and his office as high constable of the borough in 1860, with two sons working as moulders. Both censuses understate his age by about eight years, which is why the guardianship record, not the FamilySearch tree's match alone, is relied on for his parentage. Jacob Meyer's baptism (Egypt Reformed, 2 September 1781, sponsors Jacob and Gertrude Kern) and the generations above him, Peter Meyer Sr. and Catharina Kern and then Martin Meyer Sr., come from FamilySearch tree profiles and are not yet independently verified; Martin is marked probable. Their daughter Catharine (1833–1925) married Frederick Millet about 1862.

**Millet / Millot.** Frederick Millet (1820–1864) is documented at St. John's Reformed, Orwigsburg (deacon, 1846), in the 1850 and 1860 censuses at Tamaqua, and in Schuylkill County letters of administration of 18 July 1864 and deeds of 1865. His descent from Martin Millot of Orwigsburg rests on the 1830 census household (a boy aged 10–14) and the FamilySearch tree; no baptism has been found, since St. John's register begins in 1833. Martin and John Millot are tied together by deeds of 1816–17 for lot 201 on Perry Street, and to Berks County by the rarity of the name: the only Millot in the Berks tax lists of 1767–1785 is "Mylot, Fred'k, taylor" of Colebrookdale Township (1781), who is the Sgt. Frederick Millet of the 1780 and 1782 militia rolls (both companies were raised from the Colebrookdale–Oley district). Four people of the name born 1777–c. 1785 in the county — Maria and George of Oley, John and Martin of Orwigsburg — are treated as his probable children. Newspapers (Berks and Schuylkill Journal, 1818–1820) add John's sheriff's sales and his role as an Independent Republican committeeman in 1820. In September 2026 FamilySearch's full-text index of Berks and Schuylkill deeds, probate, and court dockets was searched for Millot, Milot, Mylot, and Miloth: it returned only the Orwigsburg deeds already known, a 1817 court docket naming John Millot, a 1856 naturalization of an unrelated Martin Millot, and the 1875 estate of Mary Millot (Joseph's wife), and no Frederick Millot under any spelling. Charles A. Millet's 1911 marriage register entry (p. 385) and his son's 2001 obituary add the Martin family.

**Nickerson.** From Willard (1927–2011) back through Stephen John (b. 1900, Coeymans), Levi (1865–1926), Stephen Smith (1833–1908), Levi (1763–1847), Eliphaz (1739–1794), James (1704–1757), Ensign William (1668–1742), William Jr. (1646–1719), to William Nickerson (1604–1690) of Norwich and Chatham, and to Anne Busby's parents Nicholas and Bridget Busby. Censuses, Find a Grave, FamilySearch, the Nickerson Family Association's history articles (the 2013 "William and Anne (Busby) Nickerson Come to Town," drawing on William C. Smith's *History of Chatham*, and Debra Lawless's 2017 "Our Ancestors' Fascinating Journey" series), and Willard's 2011 obituary. The Association's articles supply the 1637 voyage on the *Rose* or the *John and Dorothy*, the 1656 purchase of Monomoit from Mattaquason and its price, the 1672 settlement with Plymouth Colony for £90, the homestead at Ryder's Cove about 1664, the church services in William's house, and the incorporation of Chatham on 11 June 1712. The Jersey Journal (1942–1954) adds addresses, the 1948 engagement to Evelyn Christoffersen, and the 1954 marriage to Ethel Paul. The 1940 Bayonne census shows Orpha Nickerson remarried as Orpha Kerr, with her son Thomas Kerr, which explains the half-brother named in Willard's obituary.

**Christoffersen / Olsen.** Walter Alvin Christoffersen (b. 1900, Chicago) was the son of Martin Christoffersen, born January 1864 in Denmark to Hans Jacob and Karen (parish not yet found), and Inger Mathilde Olsen, born 25 December 1868 at Egersund, Norway, who married in Chicago on 7 November 1891. Inger's parents Otto Johan Olsen (b. 8 July 1845) and Olava Olsdatter (m. 18 October 1866), and Otto's parents Ole Andreas Olsen and Inger Marie Thorsdatter, come from the Egersund parish registers. Olava's parentage, which Phase 1 could only call probable, is now proved from nine Digitalarkivet records read directly: her baptism of 25 February 1849 (born 25 January, a twin, illegitimate, mother Marthe Olsdatter, a servant girl at Dyrnæs, who named Ole Olsen of Egersund as father), her confirmation of 4 October 1863 giving the same birth date and both parents, the 1865 census with her mother in Storgaden, the 1866 marriage naming her father, the baptisms of her children Ole Ingeman (1867), Ingeborg Andrea (1871), and Marthe (1874), and the 1870 and 1875 censuses with Marthe Olsdatter in the household, "supported by her son-in-law Otto Joh. Olsen" in 1875. Ole Olsen and Marthe never married. On the Jersey City side, Louise S. Mihm is identified with Sophie L. "Lulu" Mihm, born June 1890 to Joseph Mihm and Louisa Mayer, through the 1900 census (with her grandfather Jacob Mayer in the household), the 1922 city directory, her Social Security death record (b. 14 June 1890, d. September 1977), the 1950 census (widowed), and the Find a Grave index (Belvidere Cemetery).

**Martin, Williams, Baran, Mihm.** The four direct-line women researched in Phase 2 are established as follows. Mary Catherine Martin: the Pennsylvania delayed birth record (18 August 1891, Grier City, parents Adolph Martin and Catherine Herring), the 1900 and 1910 censuses of Rush Township, and the 1911 marriage register with her father's consent. Marie Violet Williams: her Social Security NUMIDENT record (parents William E. Williams and Evelyn "Berk"), the 1930 Gilberton census in which she and her brother are grandchildren in William H. and Cora Williams's household, the 1930 Allentown marriage of William E. Williams and Evelyn D. Beck (naming his parents William H. Williams and Cora Dornsife and hers, George A. and Eleanor Dutes Beck), the 1940 and 1950 Tamaqua censuses, his 1942 draft registration, and the Find a Grave entries at Highland Memorial Park. Because Evelyn was single in April 1930 and married in September, she is Marie's stepmother; the birth mother is unidentified. Julia Baran: the 1910, 1915, and 1920 Bayonne censuses (George and Mary Baran, from Hungary/Austria, with ten children), the 1930, 1940, and 1950 censuses, her daughter's NUMIDENT record, and a 1956 residence entry. Louise Mihm: as above.


## People, by line and generation

Generation numbers count back from Chris Millet (0): 1 = parents, 2 = grandparents, and so on; −1 = children. Within each line, people are listed from the earliest generation forward.


## Michelet / Mickley

### Jehan Michelet, “le Masson” (fl. 1444)

**Place:** Metz  
**Role:** Earliest recorded Michelet of Metz  
**Generation:** 13  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet; link marked probable

A mason (“le Masson”) of Metz recorded in 1444, the head of the genealogical table of the Michelet family of Metz that Prof. Karl Ludwig Michelet of Berlin compiled from city baptismal certificates, the researches of the Metz Protestant pastor, and family papers, and sent to Pennsylvania in 1883. Later generations of the table are borne out by the surviving Reformed registers; the earlier ones, in the words of the 1893 genealogy, are “more or less conjectural.” The same table names a Jean Michelet, butcher, invested with the Order of Saint Michael by Louis XI in 1471 and Seigneur de Beauval, and an Antoine Jacques Michelet, president of the Parlement of Rouen in 1596, bearing the same arms. The family's arms, as carried from Metz to Berlin, show a fortress tower topped by a cap of liberty with a stag leaping the moat, and the motto “War, the Chase and Liberty.”
 **A caution about the table itself.** Its “first generation,” presented as the children of Jehan, runs from a knighthood in 1471 to a death in 1610 — a hundred and sixty-six years. Those eight people cannot be siblings. The top of this line is a list of notable Michelets rather than a pedigree, and the generations between Jehan and Jacquemin are not established. The eight are: Jean, *le boucher*, invested in the Order of Saint-Michel by Louis XI in 1471 and Seigneur de Beauval; Antoine Jacques, Seigneur d'Interville and président au parlement de Rouen in 1596; Anne Michelet de Beauval, who married Charles de la Ville Auffrai, baron de Paynel-Mercy, in 1615; Claude, *hoste* at Metz in 1482; Etienne *le Bolangier*, 1542; Claude *l'escripvain*, 1565; Jacquemin himself; and a sister Marguerite, wife of Claude Estienne, 1572.
- Descendant, intermediate generations omitted: Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet (probable)

Sources:

- [Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America (1893)](https://archive.org/stream/cu31924029842865/cu31924029842865_djvu.txt)
- [Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America (1893), “European Genealogy — La Famille Michelet de Metz, d'après des documents authentiques”: the numbered table sent from Berlin in 1883, generations 1–8](https://archive.org/stream/cu31924029842865/cu31924029842865_djvu.txt)

### Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet (c. 1550–1610)

**Place:** Metz  
**Role:** Clerk to the King's treasurer at Metz  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

A scrivener (escripvain) of Metz in 1576, clerk in 1579, receiver (receveur) in 1587, and from 1593 clerk to the King's treasurer; died in 1610. Twice married in the Reformed church: first to Suzanne Joly, who died in 1584, and on July 28, 1591, to Suzanne Wiriot. By his first wife he had Jacques (1576–1651, an elder of the church), Susanne, Pierre (1582–1632, a pastor), Paul (1584, deacon and farmer of the city mills, whose sons went to Norway), and Daniel; by the second, Samuel and Esther, who married David de la Cloche.
 The Berlin table gives his career as a sequence of offices — *escripvain* in 1576, *clerc* in 1579, *receveur* in 1587, and *commis du trésorier du Roy*, clerk to the King's treasurer, by 1593 — and his death in 1610. It also gives him **two wives**, which most trees miss: Suzanne Joly, who died in 1584 leaving four children, and then Suzanne Wiriot, married in 1591, by whom he had Daniel, Samuel, and Esther. The American line descends from the second marriage.
- Children (linked in tree): Daniel Michelet; Paul Michelet
- Ancestor, intermediate generations omitted: Jehan Michelet, “le Masson” (probable)
- Spouses: Suzanne Joly (d. 1584); Suzanne Wiriot, m. 28 Jul 1591
- Children: Jacques (1576), Susanne (1579), Pierre (1582), Paul (1584), Daniel (1592), Samuel (1598), Esther (1603)

### Daniel Michelet (1592)

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Baptized in the Reformed church of Metz on September 20, 1592, son of Jacquemin Michelet. Married Marie Colin, daughter of Daniel Collin, a practitioner at the law courts (praticien du palais), on February 8, 1615. His eldest son Daniel (1615–1659) married Judith de la Cloche; his daughter Esther died unmarried in 1684; his son Jacques, the dyer, was grandfather of the emigrant to Pennsylvania.

- Parents: Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet
- Children (linked in tree): Jacques Michelet
- Spouse: Marie Colin, m. 8 Feb 1615
- Children: Daniel (1615–1659), Esther (1619–1684), Jacques (1623–1685), Suzanne (1627–1668)

### Paul Michelet (1584)

**Place:** Metz → (sons to Norway, 1644)  
**Role:** Ancestor of the Norwegian Michelets  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

Baptized December 16, 1584, son of Jacquemin Michelet and Suzanne Joly; a deacon of the Reformed church and farmer of the city mills of Metz. Married Anne Pillon, daughter of Josué Pillon, receiver of the bullette tax. Two of his sons, Paul (baptized September 5, 1617) and Jacques (baptized April 7, 1619), emigrated to Norway in 1644 as officers in the Danish-Norwegian army; Paul was a lieutenant in 1644 and major by 1658, present at the siege of Trondheim, and died in 1659. Their descendants became the Norwegian Michelet family of soldiers, clergy, and jurists, among them Major General Jørgen Michelet (1742–1818) and Major General Christian Frederik Michelet (1792–1874), commandant of Fredriksten fortress; a Captain Michelet of Trondheim was corresponding with the Berlin branch in the 1880s.
 The table names his wife — **Anne, daughter of Josué Pillon, receveur des deniers de la bullette** — and his position: deacon, and *fermier des moulins de la ville*, tenant of the town mills. His sons Paul and Jacques are the ones who actually crossed to Norway in 1644. FamilySearch's index dates the marriage — 12 May 1613, to Anne Pillon — and gives him a death at Metz in April 1668, neither of which the table records.
- Parents: Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet
- Spouse: Anne Pillon
- Children: Paul (1617–1659), Jacques (1619)

### Jacques Michelet (1623–1685)

**Place:** Metz, Lorraine  
**Role:** Dyer and deacon of the Reformed church of Metz  
**Generation:** 10  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born in Metz and baptized in its Reformed church on December 28, 1623, son of Daniel Michelet and Marie Colin. A dyer (teinturier) by trade and a deacon of the Protestant congregation. Married Anne Philpin at Metz on October 30, 1646; the family table compiled in Berlin carries an entry for an Anne, died 1681, “veuve Dubois,” which is probably how the name Du Bois came to be attached to her in some later trees. Metz had been a free city of the Holy Roman Empire until France seized it in 1552, and its large Reformed community kept its own registers from 1561 until the Revocation. Anne died in 1681. Jacques was buried on October 8, 1685, days before the Edict of Fontainebleau outlawed Protestant worship in France that same month. Of his children, Pierre became a mercer and deacon; the youngest, Louis, born when his father was fifty-two, carried the line to Zweibrücken and America.
 The printed table settles the Du Bois question outright: the Anne styled *veuve Dubois*, died 1681, is entered there **as his daughter**, alongside Pierre the mercer and deacon, Jacques the dyer, and Louis — not as his wife.
- Parents: Daniel Michelet
- Children (linked in tree): Louis Michelet
- Spouse: Anne Philpin (c. 1630–1681), m. 30 Oct 1646
- Children: Anne (1649), Pierre (1652), Jacques (1657), Judith (1659), Louis (1675)

### Louis Michelet (1675–1750)

**Place:** Metz → Zweibrücken  
**Role:** Merchant, later Protestant pastor  
**Generation:** 9  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born in Metz on December 17, 1675, and baptized in the Reformed church there, the youngest child of Jacques Michelet and Anne Philpin; he was nine when the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes closed the Metz temple. A merchant. Because no Protestant marriage could be solemnized in French Metz, he married Suzanne Mangeot (born June 26, 1674) in 1697 at Zweibrücken (Deux-Ponts), the Palatine duchy just over the border, and the couple stayed there until their first son was born and baptized; Suzanne's own diary, still kept by the Berlin branch of the family in 1883, records this. The Metz archives list Louis and Suzanne in 1699 as “new converts” openly declaring for the Reformed faith, and in 1715 Louis, by then a widower, declaring his five surviving children Protestant. He later served as a Protestant pastor at Zweibrücken and died there on February 27, 1750. His children scattered with the Huguenot diaspora: Jean Jacques to Pennsylvania, Marie and Louis to Berlin, Jeanne out of Metz in 1708 in the household of the Countess of Nassau. Metz holds baptism certificates for four of the children — Barbe, born 16 November 1702; Marie, 11 December 1703; Louis, 8 December 1705, who left for Berlin in 1720; and Pierre, 21 December 1710 — and none for the eldest. That gap is the whole difficulty of the next generation.

- Parents: Jacques Michelet
- Children (linked in tree): Louis Michelet; Jean Jacques Michelet (John Jacob Mickley)
- Spouse: Suzanne Mangeot (1674–bef. 1715), m. 1697 at Zweibrücken
- Children: Jean Jacques (1697), Jeanne (1699), Barbe (1702), Marie (1703, to Berlin, m. Pierre Perrin), Louis (1705–1766, to Berlin), Pierre (1710)

### Jean Jacques Michelet (John Jacob Mickley) (1697–1769)

**Place:** Zweibrücken → Oley, Berks Co. → Egypt, Whitehall Twp., PA  
**Role:** Immigrant, 1733  
**Generation:** 8  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Called the eldest son of Louis Michelet and Suzanne Mangeot, born at Zweibrücken in 1697 while his parents were there to marry. **This descent is reasoned, not recorded, and is the weakest join in the tree; it is marked probable.** What the Metz archives give is a declaration of 1715 in which Louis, a widower, entered five children as Protestants: Jean, Barbe, Marie, Louis, and Pierre. Baptism certificates survive at Metz for the four younger ones and not for the eldest, and Karl Ludwig Michelet had already put a question mark against the name Jean in his own genealogical table before any American wrote to him. When Minnie Mickley told him in 1883 of a Pennsylvania ancestor born about 1697, he decided the omission “plainly explains itself” — the couple had stayed at Zweibrücken for the birth of their first child, which is also what Suzanne Mangeot’s diary says — and called the identification “in the highest degree probable,” resting it on finding no other Jean in the whole table who could have been born near that year. Two things sit against it. The 1715 declaration names the son **Jean**, not Jean Jacques. And the Zweibrücken registers were searched in person in 1869, at the family’s request, with the reply that nothing could be found to show that such a family had ever resided there — the record, if it existed, being lost. Karl Michelet himself thought writing to Zweibrücken almost hopeless but advised trying.

A check of FamilySearch on 7 September 2026 weakens the argument further. The four certificates Karl weighed against the missing fifth are Metz records of 1702–1710, and Protestant worship at Metz ended with the Revocation in 1685: the Reformed registers stop there, and after that date the children of *nouveaux convertis* were entered in Catholic parish registers, priests before 1792 keeping records for Catholics alone. Those four are therefore Catholic or civil entries made while the family lived in the city — which it did only from about 1699, the year of their declaration. A child born at Zweibrücken in 1697 would be absent from them whatever became of him. The gap Karl believed the American account explained required no explaining at all, and the identification is left resting on his elimination argument alone. Sailed from Rotterdam on the ship Hope of London, Daniel Reid master, by way of Cowes, and landed at Philadelphia on August 28, 1733, one of 389 Palatine passengers; the list spells him Johan Jacob Mückli, and he took the oath of allegiance the same week. A single man on arrival, he lived several years with Jacob Levan, a Huguenot relative, in the Oley valley of Berks County, then settled at Egypt in Whitehall Township, now Lehigh County, buying land from Adam Deshler by deed of December 14, 1761, on a tract William Penn had first granted in 1681. The name passed through Michelet, Miquelet, Mückli, and Michley before settling as Mickley; the village of Mickleys stands on his original tract, 450 acres of which were still in the family in 1893. Two of his children, Henry and Barbara, were killed in a raid on October 8, 1763. Died August 18, 1769; his wife died the same month. Their slate stones having worn away, descendants reinterred them at St. John's Union (Mickleys) Cemetery under a monument raised by subscription in 1864.

- Parents: Louis Michelet
- Children (linked in tree): John Martin Mickley; John Peter Mickley; Henry Mickley; Barbara Mickley; Pvt. John Jacob Mickley Jr.
- Spouse: Elizabeth Barbara Burkhalter (1719–1769)
- Children: John Jacob (1737), Mary Magdalena (1744), John Martin (1745), John Peter (1752), Henry (1754), Barbara (1756), Susanne

### Louis Michelet (1705–1766)

**Place:** Metz → Berlin  
**Role:** Silk manufacturer in Berlin  
**Generation:** 8  
**Status:** relative

Born at Metz December 8, 1705, younger brother of the emigrant to Pennsylvania. Left Metz for Berlin in 1720, arriving with David Girard, a kinsman by marriage, to apprentice in the silk manufactory of Girard, Michelet & Compagnie, in which he later became a partner; the firm stayed in the family for three generations. Berlin's French colony, founded by Huguenot refugees after 1685, was where the Michelets of Prussia married only within the faith. Died 1766. His son Pierre (1741–1787) was an officer of hussars under Frederick the Great; his grandson Edouard a captain in the war of 1813–15; his great-grandson the philosopher Karl Ludwig Michelet.

- Parents: Louis Michelet
- Descendant, intermediate generations omitted: Prof. Karl Ludwig Michelet
- Children: Louis (1736–1800), Marie Françoise (1738–1796), Pierre (1741–1787), Robert David (1744–1802)

### Maj. Paul Michelet (1617–1659)

**Place:** Metz → Norway  
**Role:** Major; to Norway, 1644  
**Generation:** 10  
**Status:** relative

Baptised at Metz 5 September 1617, elder son of Paul Michelet the deacon and miller. He left for Norway in 1644 with his brother Jacques and took a commission there the same year, rising from lieutenant in 1644 to major in 1658; he died in 1659. He and his brother are the origin of the **Norwegian Michelets**, the army family that produced officers and generals into the nineteenth century — the branch the 1893 book describes only in passing.
 **This is the one point where the Berlin table has been checked against an outside source and held.** Norwegian accounts of the family, written independently, give the same origin — two Huguenot brothers from Metz — and the same career for Paul: emigrated 1644, lieutenant 1644, major 1658. They put his death in 1660 against the table's 1659. It is a small corroboration, but until now every statement above Jean Jacques rested on the 1883 document alone.
- Parents: Paul Michelet
- Career: Lieutenant 1644; Major 1658

### Pvt. John Jacob Mickley Jr. (1737–1808)

**Place:** Egypt, Whitehall Twp., Lehigh Co., PA  
**Role:** Hauled the Liberty Bell, 1777  
**Generation:** 7  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born December 17, 1737. Married Susanne Margarethe Miller in 1760. Elected to the Northampton County Committee of the Revolution in November 1776, and gave the use of his horses and wagons to the Continental Army throughout the war. In September 1777, after the Battle of Brandywine, the Executive Council ordered Philadelphia's bells out of the city so the British could not melt them into cannon; the State House bell, the Christ Church chimes, and St. Peter's bell, eleven in all, were carried off at night under the appearance of farm wagons. The Liberty Bell rode on his wagon, drawn by his own horses, with his eleven-year-old son beside him. The Moravian diary at Bethlehem records on September 23, 1777, that “the wagon with the State House Bell broke down here, so it had to be unloaded,” and neighbor Frederick Leaser's wagon finished the haul to Zion Reformed Church in Allentown, where the bell lay under the floor until June 1778. Pastor Abraham Blumer, who helped hide it, later became father-in-law to his daughter Sarah. He was killed by a tree falling on him near his home at Mickleys, December 12, 1808. A plaque at Zion Reformed (1908) and a family memorial at St. John's Union Cemetery (1917) commemorate him.

### Pvt. John Jacob Mickley III (1766–1857)

**Place:** Mickleys, Lehigh Co., PA  
**Role:** Rode on the bell wagon, age 11  
**Generation:** 6  
**Status:** relative

Born April 13 (or 14), 1766, eldest son of John Jacob Jr. At eleven he rode on the wagon bearing the Liberty Bell from Philadelphia to Allentown in 1777 and was occasionally allowed to drive; the story of that first trip to the city, told to his grandchildren, was still repeated in the family in 1893. Marched with the state militia to western Pennsylvania in the Whiskey Rebellion of 1794. Married Eva Catherine Schreiber. Died in the homestead at Mickleys on April 1, 1857, at ninety. His sons included Jacob, who kept the homestead, and Joseph, the Philadelphia numismatist.

### Joseph J. Mickley (1799–1878)

**Place:** Catasauqua → Philadelphia  
**Role:** “Father of American numismatics”  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** relative

Joseph Jacob Mickley, born March 24, 1799, at Catasauqua, son of John Jacob III, the boy on the bell wagon. Went to Philadelphia in 1811, apprenticed as a piano maker, and built up his own instrument business, taking a Franklin Institute prize in 1831. From 1842 to 1869 his house at 927 Market Street was a gathering place for collectors, historians, and musicians. He assembled the finest coin collection in America of its day, with autographs of the signers of the Declaration and every president to Grant; a burglary on April 13, 1867, led him to sell most of the coins at auction that autumn. First president of the Numismatic and Antiquarian Society of Philadelphia, founded 1857. Fluent in French, German, and Swedish, he toured the mints of Europe from 1869 to 1872 gathering specimens for the United States Mint, and wrote on the Indian raids in Pennsylvania (1875). Died in Philadelphia February 15, 1878.

- Parents: Pvt. John Jacob Mickley III
- Spouses: Cordelia Hopfeldt, m. 1825; Diana Blumer
- Children: Six, incl. Henry Joseph, Josephine, Hannah, John Jacob (1836–1892)

### Prof. Karl Ludwig Michelet (1801–1893)

**Place:** Berlin  
**Role:** Hegelian philosopher; compiled the Metz family table  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** relative

Born in Berlin December 4, 1801, son of Louis Guillaume Michelet (1775–1841) and Wilhelmine Victoire Girard, of the Huguenot French colony. Doctor of philosophy at Berlin in 1824, professor from 1829 until his death; a pupil of Hegel who edited Hegel's collected works (1832–42), founded the Berlin Philosophical Society in 1845, and wrote on ethics, Aristotle, and the personality of God. In 1883, answering a letter from Minnie F. Mickley of Pennsylvania, he sent the genealogical table of the Michelets of Metz from 1444, reasoned out that her ancestor Jean Jacques must be the missing eldest son of Louis Michelet and Suzanne Mangeot born at Zweibrücken, and concluded that the American and Berlin families descended from two brothers. Her father visited him in Berlin in 1889. Died December 15, 1893, the year the Mickley genealogy was printed.

- Ancestor, intermediate generations omitted: Louis Michelet
- Spouses: Marie Scholz (1813–1864); Jenny Vallon (1839–1886)
- Children: Paul, Jenny, Charles, Louisa, George, Helene, Eugene (1878–1883)

### Lt. Edwin Mickley (1830–1898)

**Place:** Mickleys, PA  
**Role:** Raised the 1864 monument  
**Generation:** 4  
**Status:** relative

Born April 20, 1830, on the homestead at Mickleys, which he owned in 1893 with the original 1789 “Oczakow” tract. First lieutenant of Company B, 38th Pennsylvania Volunteer Militia, in the emergency of 1863. When the 1864 subscription to mark the graves of the immigrant John Jacob Mickley fell short, he made up the difference and had the couple reinterred under a new tomb at Mickleys' church. Married Matilda E. Fogel about 1854; visited the Michelet cousins in Berlin in 1889. Died September 5, 1898.

- Parents: Lt. Jacob Mickley
- Children (linked in tree): Minnie Fogel Mickley
- Spouse: Matilda E. Fogel (1834–?)
- Children: Anna Ophelia Desdemona, Lillie Ellen Eva (m. Dr. Henry Martyn Chance), Minnie Fogel, John Jacob

### Minnie Fogel Mickley (c. 1860)

**Place:** Mickleys, PA  
**Role:** Compiled the family genealogy, 1893  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** relative

Daughter of Edwin Mickley of the homestead. In 1883 she wrote to Prof. Karl Ludwig Michelet in Berlin and drew from him the Metz family table and the diary evidence that fixed her ancestor's birthplace at Zweibrücken. Her “Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America” (Newark, 1893), dedicated to Michelet and to the memory of John Jacob Mickley, records 503 American descendants, the family's war service, the Liberty Bell episode as told in the family, and the Michelets of Metz, Berlin, and Norway; she also wrote the account of the bell for the American Monthly Magazine.

- Parents: Lt. Edwin Mickley

Sources:

### Thomas Moyer (1804–1869)

**Place:** South Whitehall Twp. → Tamaqua, PA  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born November 19, 1804, in Whitehall Township, then Northampton County, and baptized at Egypt Reformed Church on January 26, 1805, as Thomas Mayer, son of Jacob Mayer and Christina, in the same congregation where the Mickleys were baptized. His father died when he was thirteen: at the February term of 1818 his mother petitioned the Lehigh County Orphans' Court as widow of Jacob Moyer, yeoman, of South Whitehall, and the court gave Thomas and his brother Stephen into the guardianship of Peter Moyer, presumably his grandfather or uncle of that name. Married Susanna Mickley on August 6, 1833, in South Whitehall Township, and moved to Tamaqua, in the coal region, where the family was living in the east ward in 1850, the enumerator recording him as a potter, and in 1860, when at 48 (his age understated in both censuses) he was high constable of the borough with two sons working as moulders. Nine children born between 1833 and 1857. Died June 2, 1869, in Tamaqua; his son Albert died the same year. Recorded as Moyer, Mayer, Meyer, and Meyers.

- Parents: Jacob Meyer; Christina Newan Meyer
- Spouse (linked in tree): Susanna Mickley Moyer
- Children (linked in tree): Cpl. Zachary Taylor Moyer; Catharine A. Moyer Millet; Emma E. Moyer Hinkley

Born November 21, 1833, in South Whitehall Township, Lehigh County, eldest child of Thomas Moyer and Susanna Mickley. Married the widower Frederick Millet in Tamaqua about 1861–62; their only child, Charles, was born in 1863, and Frederick died the following year. ## Millet / Millot

### Sgt. Frederick Millet (Mylot, Miloth) (fl. 1780–82)

**Place:** Colebrookdale Twp., Berks Co., PA  
**Role:** Tailor; militia sergeant, 1780 and 1782  
**Generation:** 6  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet; link marked probable

A tailor taxed in Colebrookdale Township, Berks County, in 1781 (“Mylot, Fred'k, taylor”), the only person of the name in the county's surviving tax lists of 1767–1785. Served twice as a sergeant of the Berks County militia during the Revolution: in Captain Henry Strouch's company of Colonel Joseph Hiester's Sixth Battalion, on active duty from August 10 to September 9, 1780, and in Captain Abraham Keefer's company mustered September 10, 1782, where his fellow sergeants were Abraham Lesher of the Oley ironmaking family and Michael Kooser. Both companies were raised from the Colebrookdale and Oley district south-east of Reading: the Kussers, Eckerts, Mattes, and Yoxheimers on their rolls are his neighbors on the Colebrookdale list. Colebrookdale and Oley were settled in part by Huguenot families, and the Millot/Milot name is French. He is not found in the 1790 or 1800 census indexes under any spelling, and no Millot appears in the Brunswick Township (Orwigsburg) lists of 1779–1785, so the family's move north came later. Four people of this rare surname, born 1777 to about 1785 in the same county, fit as his children: Maria (1777) and George (1782), who stayed in Oley, and John (about 1781) and Martin, who were in Orwigsburg by 1815. None of these links is yet proved by a baptism or will. FamilySearch's full-text index of Berks and Schuylkill deeds and probate records contains no Frederick Millot, Milot, Mylot, or Miloth; the baptisms, if they survive, are in the Oley and Colebrookdale church registers of the 1770s and 1780s, which are not indexed online.

- Children (linked in tree): Martin Millot (probable); John Millot (probable); George Milot (Melot) (probable); Maria Milot Liess (probable)
- **1767, Longswamp Township — the earliest record of the name in Pennsylvania.** On **27 July 1767** Thomas Groom applied to the Land Office for a warrant for 150 acres "adjoining the Land of Nicholas Harm, **Frederick Millet** & Jacob Weiss, in **Long Swamp Township Berks County**." Frederick is named as an *adjoining landowner*, so he held land in Longswamp thirteen years before the militia roll and fourteen before the Colebrookdale tax list. Two things follow. He was an adult landholder by 1767, so **born by about 1746** — which makes him thirty-four or more in 1780, a plausible age for a sergeant with children from 1777. And since the 1781 assessment taxes him as a tailor **with no valuation**, he was landless by then: between 1767 and 1781 he left or lost the Longswamp land, and that transfer should have left a **Berks County deed**. Source: FamilySearch full-text, *Applications for warrants: Pennsylvania. Warrants 1767*, image 564 of 1,237, [ark:/61903/3:1:3QHV-V385-D98B-4](https://www.familysearch.org/ark:/61903/3:1:3QHV-V385-D98B-4). Found 10 September 2026 only because the "Millet" spelling was searched for the first time.
- **The dates now collide, and that is the most useful thing to come out of this.** Frederick held land in Longswamp in **July 1767**, so he was of age, so he was **born in 1746 or earlier**. Nicklauß Möloth landed at Philadelphia on **9 October 1749**. *Frederick's birth therefore precedes the arrival of the only candidate father by at least three years.* Two readings, and both are informative:
  - **Möloth is his father, and Frederick was born in Europe.** He would have been three to six years old in 1749 and so under sixteen, which is exactly why he is not on the Lydia's oath list — those listed only men of sixteen and over. This reading fits everything else: the Germanised surname, the German neighbours, the fact that he already had land within eighteen years of landing. **But it puts his baptism in a German register — Zweibrücken, Württemberg, Durlach or the Palatinate, the Lydia's four home regions — not in a Pennsylvania one.**
  - **Or Frederick was born in Pennsylvania to some other man of the name.** That requires a Millot family in the colony before about 1746, and Strassburger records no such arrival at Philadelphia in the whole span 1727–1775 apart from Möloth in 1749. It would mean entry through another port, before 1727, or as a woman or child leaving no oath record.
  **Either way, the conclusion is the same and it explains the dead ends: every Pennsylvania church register searched so far — Longswamp Reformed, Oley, Colebrookdale — has been searched for a baptism that probably was never entered in Pennsylvania at all.** The parents are most likely to be found in a German register, and the Lydia's passenger origins are the shortlist of where to look.
- **The 1767 warrant is a manuscript, and it spells him *Millet*.** This is stronger evidence than the 1780 pay roll, which is a printed transcription of 1906: here the clerk's own hand writes *Frederick Millet* in 1767. The claim that the family was Millot, Mylot or Miloth in every record before the 1840s is therefore **wrong**, and not merely as an editor's slip.
- 1780 company: Capt. Henry Strouch; Lt. Jacob Kester; Ens. John Eckert; fellow sergeants Christopher Berninger, Andrew Haas. The roll is a **pay** roll, and it spells him **"Frederick Millet"** — the modern spelling, in 1780
- 1782 company: Capt. Abraham Keefer; Lt. Charles Eckart; Ens. Christopher Wentz; fellow sergeants Abraham Lesher and Michael Kooser. Also a pay roll: **"Frederick Miloth," Sergt., time of entry Sept. 10, service 1 month 14 days** — the same term as Kooser and Lt. Eckart, where the captain, the ensign, Lesher and the corporals served 1 month 23 days
- 1782 company: Capt. Abraham Keefer; Lt. Charles Eckart; Ens. Christopher Wentz; fellow sergeants Abraham Lesher, Michael Kooser
- Probable children: Maria (1777), John (c. 1781), George (1782), Martin

### George Milot (Melot) (1782–1862)

**Place:** Oley Twp., Berks Co., PA  
**Role:** Cattle drover of Oley  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** relative; link marked probable

Born April 24, 1782. A “cattledriver” of Oley Township, the Huguenot-settled valley next to Colebrookdale where the tailor Frederick Mylot was taxed the year before George's birth. Married Susanna Sayer (Seyer, 1791–1873) and named as executor in the estate of Daniel Seyer. Twelve children born 1812–1833; his daughter Susanna died at 22 in 1843 and his son Seyer as a Union private in 1863. Head of household in Oley in every census from 1820 to 1860. Died April 16, 1862; buried at Friedens Church Cemetery, Oley. The Berks family spelled the name Milot or Melot while the Orwigsburg branch wrote Millot and, later, Millet.

### John Millot (c. 1781–bef. 1830)

**Place:** Orwigsburg, Schuylkill Co., PA  
**Role:** Independent Republican committeeman, 1820  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** relative; link marked probable

Married Elizabeth Noaker (Noecker) about 1810; their five children were baptized at Zion's “Old Red” Union Church near Orwigsburg between 1811 and 1817. Held lot 201 in Orwigsburg with Frederick G. Fidler from 1815, sold his share in June 1815, and joined Fidler in conveying the lot to Martin Millot in September 1816; bought lot 74 at a sheriff's sale for $2.25 as highest bidder about 1819. His own affairs went badly: a three-acre tract of his in Brunswick Township, adjoining Wendel Seib and John Featherman, was advertised for sheriff's sale in August and September 1818, and in May 1820 the sheriff advertised his half lot on the Centre turnpike in Orwigsburg, 52 feet 6 inches wide and 11 perches deep, with a two-story frame dwelling and stable, bounded by John Kelly and Jacob Reinheimer, to be sold at Michael Graeff's inn on June 19, 1820, “late the estate of John Millot”; the neighboring lot of John Dell had gone the same way that March. He was active in the Hiester party all the same: at the Independent Republicans' Fourth of July celebration at Christian Deibert's spring in 1819 a Mr. Millot, John or Martin, gave the toast “Independent Republicanism expects every man to do his duty in the moment of trial” to six cheers, and at the county meeting of August 19, 1820, held at the Orwigsburg court-house to back General Joseph Hiester for governor, John Millot and John Kolb were named the committee of vigilance for the borough. Head of a household in Orwigsburg in the 1820 census, gone by 1830. Elizabeth afterward married Joseph Snyder and died January 15, 1876, buried at St. John's, Orwigsburg. The lot transactions tie him to Martin, and the two are most likely brothers.

- Parents: Sgt. Frederick Millet (Mylot, Miloth) (probable)
- Children (linked in tree): Joseph Millet
- Siblings (linked in tree): Martin Millot (probable)

- Children: Carolina (1811), Johan Wilhelm (1812), Maria (1814), Sarah (1816), Joseph (1817)
- Property: Lot 201 (with Fidler, 1815–16); lot 74 (c. 1819); half lot on the Centre turnpike (sold by sheriff, 1820); 3 acres in Brunswick Twp. (sold by sheriff, 1818)

### Martin Millot (c. 1780s)

**Place:** Orwigsburg, Schuylkill Co., PA  
**Role:** Lot-holder on Perry Street; head of household 1820–1830  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet; link marked probable

Married to Catharine. First seen in Orwigsburg on March 28, 1816, witnessing a deed of Jacob Orwig, of the family that founded the town. The Millots were Independent Republicans, the Hiester party: at the party's Fourth of July celebration at Christian Deibert's spring in 1819 a Mr. Millot, John or Martin, offered a toast that drew six cheers. On September 21, 1816, Frederick G. Fidler and John Millot, who had held it jointly since 1815, conveyed to him lot 201 on the general plan of the borough, on Perry Street between two alleys, 111 feet 9 inches by 45 feet 4½ inches; on November 18, 1817, Martin and Catharine sold it for thirty-five dollars to Frederick Brintzinghoffer, a coach-maker of Berks County, both signing (Martin by mark). Head of one of the two Millot households in Orwigsburg in the 1820 census, the other being John's, and of the only one left in 1830: a man and a woman in their forties, a young woman in her teens, and one boy aged 10 to 14, the bracket a son born in 1820 falls in. The FamilySearch tree names him as Frederick's father, but no baptism has been found; St. John's register begins only in 1833. After 1830 he does not appear in Schuylkill County under Millot or Millet, and may be indexed as Miller. An Irish-born Martin Millett who bought a lot in Mahanoy City in the 1860s is a different man.

- Parents: Sgt. Frederick Millet (Mylot, Miloth) (probable)
- Children (linked in tree): Frederick Millet (probable)
- Siblings (linked in tree): John Millot (probable)
- Spouse: Catharine
- Households: 1820 (p. 31) and 1830 (p. 83), Orwigsburg
- Property: Lot 201, Perry Street, Orwigsburg, 1816–1817

### Frederick Millet (1820–1864)

**Place:** Orwigsburg → Tamaqua, PA  
**Role:** Deacon, St. John's Reformed, Orwigsburg  
**Generation:** 4  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born 1820 in Orwigsburg. Married Sophia Brock about 1845. Elected a deacon of St. John's Reformed Church in Orwigsburg “to serve 2 years,” under Pastor J. W. Hoffmeier, and a communicant there on July 7 and November 8, 1846; his daughter Mary Louisa was baptized there August 29, 1846. Moved to Tamaqua by 1850 (south ward, household 186). Sophia died in 1860, leaving him a widower with son William. He bought a lot in Tamaqua, married Catharine Moyer about 1861–62, and died intestate before July 18, 1864, when his widow took out letters of administration; two 1865 deeds settled his property. No Civil War service record has been found under Millet, Millett, or Miller. No baptismal record has been found; his descent from Martin Millot rests on the 1830 household and the FamilySearch tree. The Zion (Red Church) register for 1818–1822, where John Millot's children were entered, is not indexed on FamilySearch, and neither it nor the St. John's confirmation lists of the mid-1830s have yet been read for him.

- Parents: Martin Millot (probable)
- Spouse (linked in tree): Catharine A. Moyer Millet; Sophia Brock Millet
- Children (linked in tree): Charles A. Millet; Mary Louisa Millet Shugg; George F. Millet; William C. Millet
- 1st spouse: Sophia Brock (1826–1860), m. c. 1845
- 2nd spouse: Catharine A. Moyer (1833–1925)
- Children: Mary Louisa (1846), George F. (1850), William C. (1851); Charles A. (1863)

### Frederick “Butch” Millet (1920–2016)

**Place:** Tamaqua, PA → South Bound Brook, NJ  
**Role:** 310th Field Artillery Bn., 79th Infantry Division  
**Generation:** 2  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born April 26, 1920, in Tamaqua; graduated from Tamaqua High School. Attended Citizens' Military Training Camp for three summers at Fort Meade and Fort Hoyle, Maryland, in the last years of horse-drawn artillery. Worked at Abe Sherman's Army-Navy store in Tamaqua and then at American Cyanamid (Calco) in New Jersey; heard the news of Pearl Harbor at the Majestic Theater in Tamaqua, where his future wife was an usherette. Drafted October 1942, reported to Fort Meade November 9, 1942; Camp Blanding, where he was one of the few in his unit to finish the 79th Division's Ranger training; maneuvers in Tennessee, Arizona, and Kansas; overseas from Camp Myles Standish in 1944. Normandy (the 79th landed at Utah Beach June 12–14), Cherbourg, the Falaise pocket, a night crossing of the Seine on a catwalk with the 313th Infantry, Belgium and Holland, the Hatten-Rittershoffen battle in January 1945, the Rhine at Remagen, the Ruhr, Würzburg, Pilsen; treated at a general hospital in Verdun. Home November 1945. Married Marie Violet Williams; 62 years together in South Bound Brook. Chemical shop steward at American Cyanamid; VFW Posts 12 and 2290; Reformed Church of South Bound Brook. Recorded his war for the Library of Congress on December 3, 2002. Died January 7, 2016, at 95; buried at Lake Nelson Memorial Park, Piscataway.

- Parents: Charles A. Millet; Mary C. Martin Millet
- Spouse (linked in tree): Marie Violet Williams Millet
- Children (linked in tree): Frederick W. Millet; Diane Millet Sladek; Marcia Millet Linico; Sharon Kay Millet Johnson; Marianne Millet Harris
- Spouse: Marie Violet Williams (1922–2004)
- Children: Frederick W., Diane, Marcia, Sharon Kay, Marianne

### Frederick W. Millet (d. 2016)

**Place:** South Bound Brook, NJ → Mountain Top, Luzerne Co., PA  
**Generation:** 1  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Eldest child and only son of Frederick and Marie Millet, and the third Frederick in a row on this line. Kept the two German bayonets his father brought home from the Seine crossing. Married Christine Ruth Nickerson; four children. Later married Lynette Van Fleet. Died March 3, 2016, in Mountain Top, of injuries from a motor vehicle accident the day before, eight weeks after his father. At his request there was no viewing or funeral; family and friends gathered at III Guys in Mountain Top to remember him.

## Nickerson

### William Nickerson (1604–1690)

**Place:** Norwich, England → Chatham, MA  
**Role:** Founder of Chatham  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born in England in 1603 or 1604; a weaver of Norwich, Norfolk. Married Anne Busby there on June 24, 1627, and had four children in Norwich. Puritan Congregationalists, the family left to escape religious persecution: the Family Association's history has them sailing on April 15, 1637, with Anne's parents Nicholas and Bridget Busby and about a hundred others, on either the Rose or the John and Dorothy, two ships under a father and son, and landing at Salem on June 20. Five more children were born around Boston. By 1640 they were among the first settlers of Yarmouth on Cape Cod. In 1656 he bought about a thousand acres, four square miles of Monomoit, from the sachem Mattaquason for a shallop, ten coats, six kettles, twelve axes, twelve hoes, twelve knives, forty shillings in wampum, a hat, and twelve shillings in coin. He had not asked Plymouth Colony's leave, and sixteen years of dispute followed before he settled the matter in 1672 for ninety pounds. About 1664, aged about sixty, he built his homestead near the head of Ryder's Cove, deeded land to his grown children, who settled around the cove with their families in low cottages banked with dried seaweed, and held church services in his own house, the beginning of Chatham's Congregational church; in William Smith's phrase, for the next twenty-five years the place was “but little more than a Nickerson neighborhood.” He wanted the constablewick made a town, which required a resident minister and the population to support one; his children and grandchildren saw it done on June 11, 1712, when Monomoit was incorporated as Chatham. He and Anne lived in the homestead until their deaths in the late 1680s. By 1790 twenty of Chatham's 193 households were Nickersons; by 1915 his descendants were reckoned at fifty thousand.

Savage, consulted in September 2026, adds detail to the crossing and conflicts with the Family Association on two points. He has William as a weaver of Norwich, aged 33 in 1637 — a birth about 1604, as the tree already carries — with his wife Ann, eldest daughter of Nicholas Busby, aged 28, and the four children who crossed with them now named: Nicholas, Robert, Elizabeth, and Ann. He says they embarked at Ipswich or Yarmouth on 8 April 1637, not 15 April, and landed on 20 June at Boston, not Salem; that they went first to Watertown with Nicholas Busby; that William was admitted freeman on 2 May 1638; and that at Yarmouth he had a son Joseph, born December 1647, and a daughter Elizabeth who married Robert Eldred in October 1649. Where Savage and the Association disagree, neither has been checked against a primary record here.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Anne Busby Nickerson
- Children (linked in tree): William Nickerson Jr.
- Spouse: Anne Busby (1607–1686), m. June 24, 1627, Norwich
- Children: Ten, incl. Nicholas, Elizabeth, Robert, Anne, William
- Purchase: c. 1,000 acres of Monomoit from Mattaquason, 1656; title settled with Plymouth Colony, 1672, £90
- Homestead: Head of Ryder's Cove, Chatham, c. 1664

**Place:** Norwich, England → Yarmouth, MA

One of the four Nickerson children born at Norwich who crossed with their parents and grandparents in 1637. She married Robert Eldred of Yarmouth in the last week of October 1649 and had a son Nicholas, born 18 August 1650, who became Chatham's first representative under the new charter in 1692 and died on 30 April 1702.

### William Nickerson Jr. (1646–1719)

**Place:** Yarmouth → Chatham, MA  
**Generation:** 10  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born June 1, 1646, in Yarmouth on Cape Cod, where his parents had settled by 1640, one of the children born after the family's 1637 crossing. He was ten when his father bought Monomoit from Mattaquason in 1656 and about eighteen when the homestead went up at Ryder's Cove. Married Mercy Williams on November 30, 1668, in Chatham, so he was among the grown children to whom, as the Family Association's history tells it, William deeded land and who settled with their families around the cove. Fourteen children. He outlived the constablewick: Monomoit was incorporated as Chatham on June 11, 1712, seven years before he died there on April 7, 1719.

- Parents: William Nickerson; Anne Busby Nickerson
- Spouse (linked in tree): Mercy Williams Nickerson
- Children (linked in tree): Ensign William Nickerson
- Spouse: Mercy Williams (1643–1739), m. Nov. 30, 1668

### Ensign William Nickerson (1668–1742)

**Place:** Chatham, MA  
**Role:** Tanner; militia ensign  
**Generation:** 9  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born about 1668 in the Nickerson settlement at Monomoit, a few years after his grandfather built the homestead at Ryder's Cove, and in the year his parents married. Styled Ensign from about 1706 as leader of the militia company; he was in that office when Monomoit, which had needed a resident minister and the population to keep one before it could incorporate, became the town of Chatham on June 11, 1712, the goal his grandfather had set and, in the Family Association's telling, his children and grandchildren achieved. A tanner at Cotchpinicut (Old Harbor), supplying the boots, shoes, and leather breeches nearly every man wore at work; he also held land at Monomessett (Nickerson's) Neck from his father, and left the tannery to his sons Caleb, Nathaniel, and Eldad. Married Deliverance Lombard before 1701 and, after her death, Hannah Atwood on October 24, 1717. Died November 15, 1742; buried at People's Cemetery, Chatham.

- Parents: William Nickerson Jr.; Mercy Williams Nickerson
- Spouse (linked in tree): Deliverance Lombard Nickerson
- Children (linked in tree): James Nickerson
- Spouses: Deliverance Lombard (1680–1716); Anna Hannah Atwood (1688–1764), m. 1717
- Children: Twelve, incl. William, James, Nathaniel, Joshua, Elizabeth

### James Nickerson (1704–1757)

**Place:** Chatham, MA → Ridgefield, CT  
**Role:** Left Cape Cod for Connecticut  
**Generation:** 8  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born July 28, 1704, son of Ensign William Nickerson, in what was still the constablewick of Monomoit; it was incorporated as Chatham on June 11, 1712, when he was seven. Married Dorcas Covel there in 1729; five sons and two daughters. The first of the line to leave the Nickerson neighborhood on the Cape: he moved the family to Fairfield County, Connecticut, and died there in 1757, at Ridgefield or Danbury.

### Levi Nickerson (1763–1847)

**Place:** Ridgefield, CT → Coeymans, NY  
**Role:** Settled Coeymans by 1800  
**Generation:** 6  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born 1763 in Ridgefield, Connecticut, one of nine sons of Eliphaz Nickerson. Moved to Coeymans on the Hudson below Albany, where he appears in every census from 1800 to 1840. Married first Amy (1766–1816), then about 1819 Huldah Meech, 35 years younger; Stephen Smith was born of the second marriage when Levi was 70. Died May 9, 1847; buried at Onesquethaw Cemetery, Feura Bush.

### Stephen John Nickerson (1900)

**Place:** Coeymans, NY → Bayonne, NJ  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born April 15, 1900, in Coeymans, Albany County, New York, youngest of the three children of Levi and Orpha Nickerson; in Coeymans in the 1905 and 1910 censuses. Registered for the World War I draft at Albany in 1917–18 and appears in the Veterans Administration index. Married Julia Baran; by 1930 the family was in Bayonne, New Jersey, and in 1940 his mother Orpha was living with them. In 1942 he and Julia were registered voters at 100 West 33rd Street, Bayonne, and in 1948 the family was at 830 Avenue C. By January 1954 they had moved to Dunellen, and that June the wedding notice placed them at Warrenville, in Warren Township.

### Willard Stephen Nickerson (1927–2011)

**Place:** Coeymans Hollow, NY → Bayonne, Dunellen & Highland Park, NJ  
**Role:** Master carpenter; U.S. Navy, WWII  
**Generation:** 2  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born at Coeymans Hollow, New York, in the Albany County hamlet where four generations of his family had lived (July 1927 by a residence record, 1928 by the censuses). Grew up in Bayonne, New Jersey, with his sister Luella, and attended Bayonne High School. Served in the Navy in World War II and was honorably discharged. His engagement to Evelyn Christoffersen of 52 Bergen Avenue, Jersey City, was announced in the Jersey Journal on July 31, 1948, when his parents were at 830 Avenue C, Bayonne; in April 1950 he was a carpenter for a house builder, living in Dunellen with Evelyn. In January 1954 his engagement to Ethel E. Paul of Kearny was announced, and they were married at Old Bergen Church, Jersey City, at 4 p.m. on Saturday, June 19, 1954, with a reception at the Fairmount Hotel; Walter Garman was best man, Thomas Kerr and Alfred and Russell Paul ushered, and after a wedding trip to California the couple settled in Dunellen. He was then a contractor in Plainfield. With Ethel he had Connie and Carol, and raised the family in Highland Park. A master carpenter, builder, and painter; photographer, painter, and wood carver. Worshipful Master of Union Lodge No. 19, F&AM, and Patron of Allah Chapter 211, Order of the Eastern Star, New Brunswick. Died November 16, 2011, at Old Bridge; arrangements by Bronson & Guthlein, Milltown.

- Parents: Stephen John Nickerson; Julia T. Baran Nickerson
- Spouse (linked in tree): Evelyn Ruth Christoffersen Nickerson; Ethel E. Paul Nickerson

- 1st spouse: Evelyn Ruth Christoffersen (1930–2003), engaged Jul 1948
- 2nd spouse: Ethel E. Paul, m. 19 Jun 1954, Old Bergen Church, Jersey City
- Children: Christine, Connie, Carol
- Siblings: Luella Garman; half-brother Thomas Kerr
- Grandchildren: Brooke, Casey, Samantha, Luke, Fred, Christopher, Heather, Kyle

### Ole Olsen

**Place:** Egersund, Rogaland, Norway  
**Generation:** 6  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Named by Marthe Olsdatter as the father of her twins at Olava's baptism in February 1849, the pastor noting that “the stated father is at home in Egersund,” and again as her father at Olava's confirmation in 1863, where he is described as a gårdmann, a farmer holding his own land. He did not marry Marthe and does not appear in her household in 1865, 1870, or 1875. The 1866 marriage record calls Olava his daughter. Which of the several Ole Olsens of Egersund he was has not been established.

- Children (linked in tree): Olava Olsdatter Olsen

### Otto Johan Olsen (1845)

**Place:** Egersund, Rogaland, Norway  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born July 8, 1845, in Egersund and baptized there August 31, son of Ole Andreas Olsen and Inger Marie Thorsdatter. A cooper (bødker), like his father. Married Olava Olsdatter, 18, at Egersund church on October 18, 1866 (Eigersund parish book 1866–1878, p. 381, entry 28), the banns read on three Sundays from September 16; Svend Staalesen and Andreas Christoffersen stood as sponsors. Four children were baptized at Egersund between 1867 and 1874, his father standing sponsor for the first and the last. In 1870 the family was at Nyalmendingen 202 with Olava's mother; by 1875, at Storgade 150, he had left the cooper's trade to work as a shop assistant at the Brænderionsamlaget, the town's distillery cooperative, and was supporting his mother-in-law under his roof.

Born January 1864 in Denmark, son of Hans Jacob Christoffersen and Karen. Came to the United States in 1885. Married Inger Mathilde Olsen in a Lutheran church in Chicago on November 7, 1891. ## Spouses and allied families

### Bridget Busby

**Place:** Norwich, England → New England  
**Role:** Crossed in 1637  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Mother of Anne Busby Nickerson. Savage gives her age as 53 when the family landed on 20 June 1637, which puts her birth about 1584 and makes her some three years older than her husband. She crossed from Norwich with Nicholas and the Nickersons, settled at Watertown, and moved with him to Boston in 1646. Savage says she survived him by three or four years, so she died about 1660 or 1661. The Family Association's histories record nothing further of her.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Nicholas Busby
- Children (linked in tree): Anne Busby Nickerson; Nicholas Busby Jr.; John Busby; Abraham Busby; Catharine Busby Savory; Sarah Busby Grout
- Born: about 1584, by her stated age of 53 in 1637
- Died: about 1660–61

Sources:

- [Nickerson Family Association: “William and Anne (Busby) Nickerson Come to Town” (2013), drawing on William C. Smith, History of Chatham](https://nickersonassoc.com/2013/03/29/william-and-anne-busby-nickerson-come-to-town/)
- [Nickerson Family Association: “Our Ancestors' Fascinating Journey, Part II” (Debra Lawless, 2017): the 1637 party, incl. Nicholas and Bridget Busby](https://nickersonassoc.com/2017/05/12/our-ancestors-fascinating-journey-part-ii/)
- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Nicholas Busby

**Place:** Norwich, England → New England  
**Role:** Crossed in 1637  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

A weaver of Norwich and father of Anne Busby Nickerson. With his wife Bridget he joined his daughter's family for the 1637 crossing, one of the eight in the Nickerson party. Savage, who had not been consulted before September 2026, carries the rest of his life: he came from old Norwich, landing 20 June 1637 aged 50 — a birth about 1587 — with Bridget, 53, and four children, Nicholas, John, Abraham, and Sarah. He settled first at Watertown, was made freeman in March 1638, served as selectman in 1640 and 1641, removed to Boston in 1646, was constable there in 1649, and died 28 August 1657.

His will, dated 25 July 1657, gave “all his books of physic” to his eldest son John, then in England, and his books of divinity to Abraham. It names his eldest daughter Ann, wife of William Nickerson of Boston, weaver; his daughter Catharine Savory; his youngest daughter Sarah, wife of John Grout of Sudbury and before that of Capt. Thomas Cakebread; and his grandson Joseph, son of his son Nicholas, who had died before him. A man leaving separate libraries of physic and divinity was a reader of some means, which is the only sense of his standing the records give.

**The will has now been read** (9 September 2026), in the Suffolk County probate register, *Probate Records, Vol 1–4, 1628–1667*, at volume pages 308–311 — Ancestry's *Massachusetts Wills and Probate Records*, film 007703070, images 166–168. Page images are saved under `assets/suffolk-probate-1657-busby-will/`. What is filmed is the **record copy**, not the signed sheet: the hand is a uniform nineteenth-century roundhand running across every will in the book, and the attestation reads “By me Nicholas Busby & a seale”, the copyist noting that the original bore a signature and seal. It is nevertheless the register text, and it is what Savage worked from.

It confirms Savage on every point and adds a good deal he compressed away. The passage that matters most for this tree:

> Item I doe appointe my three sonnes that are heere in New England, that is to say my sonne Abraham Busby, & my sonne William Nickerson, & my sonne John Grout to gather up all my debts mentioned in my debt bookes …

Busby calls his two sons-in-law “sonne” without distinction, and names the daughters in their own right:

> Item I give & bequeath unto Anne Nickerson my Eldest daughter the sume of fiftie pounds to be payd in such goods as is gathered in, in seaven monethes after my decease. Item I doe give & bequeath unto my daughter Kathrine Savory, the some of fortie pounds (more then that I sent her the Last yeare) … Item I give unto Sarah Grout my youngest daughter the sume of Sixtie & five pounds …

So **Anne is fixed as the eldest daughter and as William Nickerson's wife from her father's own side**, which is what the tree had been taking on Savage's word alone. The full schedule of bequests: John Busby, “my Eldest sonne”, £70 on top of the £30 sent him the year before; Abraham £60 and, after his mother's death, the Boston dwelling house with its garden and fruit trees; Anne Nickerson £50; Kathrine Savory £40 beyond what was sent last year; Sarah Grout £65; the grandson Joseph Busby, “the Sonne of my Sonne Nicholas Busby deceased”, £20; and a granddaughter Sarah Grout £10. The wife — Bridget, unnamed in the will, only “my deare & loving wife” — is made whole executrix, with the dwelling house for life, the household stuff, plate and money, and the farm if she consents to its sale.

The books are itemised where Savage only summarised. To John, the physic: “Barrowes method, Dutch Phisicke & garden of health, Mr Cogans treatise and the Dialogue of Phisicke, Chirurgery with Phillipps Naturall Hystory”. To Abraham, the divinity: Perkins, Willet, a commentary on Romans, and two books of Cicero. The rest of divinity and history the two were to “lovingly & Brotherly divide”, except three Bibles — the thick Bible to Anne Nickerson, the best to Sarah Grout, and the one in his hamper to Kathrine Savory. His trade is there too: two looms, the one to John “in case hee come over to New England, or else to William Nickerson”, the other with warping bars, bobbins, wheels and shuttles to Abraham. That John might not come over confirms Savage's note that he was then in England.

Dated “this five and Twentieth day of July One thousand Six hundred fifty and seaven”, witnessed by **Nathaniell Woodward** and **Wm Pearse**, who deposed to it at a meeting of the magistrates on **10 September 1657**. Two small corrections follow. The will calls **John** the eldest son, not Nicholas — Nicholas Jr. was already dead, so this is presumably eldest surviving, but the tree should not call Nicholas Jr. “eldest son” flatly. And the 10 September date that had been noted as a possible **second, Plymouth County probate is more likely this deposition**, misfiled or double-indexed; that remains to be checked.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Bridget Busby
- Children (linked in tree): Anne Busby Nickerson; Nicholas Busby Jr.; John Busby; Abraham Busby; Catharine Busby Savory; Sarah Busby Grout
- Born: about 1587, by his stated age of 50 in 1637
- Died: 28 Aug 1657, Boston; will dated 25 July 1657
- Offices: freeman Mar 1638; selectman of Watertown 1640, 1641; constable of Boston 1649
- Grandson named in the will: Joseph, son of Nicholas Busby Jr.

Sources:

- [Nickerson Family Association: “William and Anne (Busby) Nickerson Come to Town” (2013), drawing on William C. Smith, History of Chatham](https://nickersonassoc.com/2013/03/29/william-and-anne-busby-nickerson-come-to-town/)
- [Nickerson Family Association: “Our Ancestors' Fascinating Journey, Part II” (Debra Lawless, 2017): the 1637 party, incl. Nicholas and Bridget Busby](https://nickersonassoc.com/2017/05/12/our-ancestors-fascinating-journey-part-ii/)
- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Anne Busby Nickerson (1607–1686)

**Place:** Norwich, England → Chatham, MA  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Eldest daughter of Nicholas and Bridget Busby of Norwich, so named in her father’s will, and about five years younger than her husband. Savage gives her age as 28 in 1637, putting her birth about 1609 rather than the 1607 the tree carries, which agrees with the Family Association’s estimate of 1608–09. Married William Nickerson in Norwich in 1627 and bore four children there. In 1637 she crossed to Salem with William, the four children, and both her parents, on the Rose or the John and Dorothy; five more children followed around Boston. She was in her mid-fifties when the family built its homestead at the head of Ryder's Cove about 1664, and lived there until her death in the late 1680s. Mother of ten.

- Parents: Nicholas Busby; Bridget Busby
- Spouse (linked in tree): William Nickerson
- Children (linked in tree): William Nickerson Jr.

- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Abraham Busby

**Place:** Norwich, England → Boston  
**Role:** Linen-weaver; crossed in 1637  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

One of the four Busby children who crossed in 1637. A linen-weaver of Boston, of the Artillery Company in 1647 and a freeman in 1650, he took his father's books of divinity under the 1657 will. He married on 23 September 1659, apparently as a second wife, Abigail, widow of Joseph Briscoe, and died on 20 March 1687.

- Parents: Nicholas Busby; Bridget Busby
- Spouse: Abigail, widow of Joseph Briscoe, m. 23 Sep 1659
- Died: 20 Mar 1687

Sources:

- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Catharine Busby Savory

**Place:** New England  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

A daughter named in her father's will of 1657 as Catharine Savory, so married by then. She is not among the four children Savage lists as crossing in 1637, and whether she was born in England or in New England is not established here.

- Parents: Nicholas Busby; Bridget Busby
- Married name: Savory, by 1657

Sources:

- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Sarah Busby Grout

**Place:** Norwich, England → Sudbury, MA  
**Role:** Crossed in 1637  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

The youngest Busby daughter, so called in her father's will, and one of the four children who crossed in 1637. She married first Capt. Thomas Cakebread and second John Grout of Sudbury — a selectman, town clerk, and captain who was seventy in 1689 and died on 25 July 1697 — and by Grout had John, Sarah, Joseph, Abigail, Jonathan, and Elizabeth.

- Parents: Nicholas Busby; Bridget Busby
- Spouses: Capt. Thomas Cakebread; John Grout of Sudbury
- Children by Grout: John, Sarah, Joseph (1649), Abigail (1655), Jonathan (1658), Elizabeth

Sources:

- [Savage, *A Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England*, vol. I (1860): Nicholas Busby of Norwich and Watertown, his offices, death, and will of 25 July 1657](https://archive.org/details/genealogicaldic01savarich)

### Suzanne Wiriot

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Second wife of Jacquemin Michelet, married at Metz on 28 July 1591, and the ancestress of the American line: her son Daniel, baptised 1592, carries the descent. Jacquemin was a widower with four children by Suzanne Joly when he married her; by her he had Daniel (1592), Samuel (1598), and Esther (1603). Nothing of her own family has been traced.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet, m. 28 Jul 1591
- Children (linked in tree): Daniel Michelet
- Children: Daniel (1592), Samuel (1598), Esther (1603)

### Suzanne Joly

**Place:** Metz  
**Role:** First wife of Jacquemin  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** relative

First wife of Jacquemin Michelet and mother of his elder children — Jacques (1576), Susanne (1579), Pierre (1582), and Paul (1584). She died in 1584. **The American line does not descend from her**, but from the second marriage; the distinction is easy to lose, because only the Berlin table records that there were two wives at all.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Jacques “Jacquemin” Michelet
- Children (linked in tree): Paul Michelet

### Marie Colin

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Married Daniel Michelet at Metz on 8 February 1615. The Berlin table names her father — **Daniel Collin, praticien du palais**, a legal practitioner at the Metz parlement — which places the Michelets among the city's Protestant office-holding families and not only its tradesmen. Her son Jacques, the dyer and deacon, carries the line.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Daniel Michelet, m. 8 Feb 1615
- Parents: Daniel Collin
- Children (linked in tree): Jacques Michelet
- Children: Daniel (1615), Esther (1619), Jacques (1623), Suzanne (1627)

### Daniel Collin

**Place:** Metz  
**Role:** Praticien du palais  
**Generation:** 12  
**Status:** relative

Father of Marie Colin and a *praticien du palais* at Metz — a practitioner before the parlement, the working end of the law rather than its bench. He is known only from the description the Berlin table gives him; nothing further has been looked for.

### Anne Pilon

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

Married Paul Michelet at Metz on 12 May 1613; her father was Josué Pillon, *receveur des deniers de la bullette*, the officer who collected the town's wine duty. Her sons Paul and Jacques left Metz for Norway in 1644 and founded the Norwegian branch. A **Rachel Pilon** married Paul's brother Samuel, so two of Jacquemin's sons married two Pilons.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Paul Michelet, m. 12 May 1613
- Parents: Josué Pillon, receveur des deniers de la bullette
- Children (linked in tree): Maj. Paul Michelet; Jacques Michelet (1619, to Norway)

### Pierre Philpin

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 11  
**Status:** relative

Father of Anne Philpin, named as such in the Metz register entry for her marriage to Jacques Michelet on 30 October 1646 — a parentage the Berlin table omits entirely. Nothing else of him has been traced. Two other Philpin women appear in the same registers and are likely his daughters or nieces: **Rachel**, who married Daniel Gaultier at Metz on 1 July 1674, and **Marie**, who married Josué Ladrague and had daughters Esther and Marie. The surname is rare enough in the Metz registers — twelve entries in all — that the three women are probably one family.

- Children (linked in tree): Anne Philpin
- Probable kin: Rachel Philpin, m. Daniel Gaultier 1674; Marie Philpin, m. Jos. Ladrague

Sources:

- [FamilySearch, France, Protestant Church Records 1536–1902: 1,073 Michelet entries, including the Metz baptisms, marriages and burials cited here](https://www.familysearch.org/search/record/results?f.collectionId=1582585&q.surname=Michelet)

### Jean Wiriot

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 13  
**Status:** relative

Father of Suzanne Wiriot, named in the Metz register entry for her marriage to Jacquemin Michelet on 28 July 1591. The Berlin table gives Suzanne no parentage at all, so he is known only from the register — and only from the record page itself: a surname search for Wiriot in the indexed collection returns nothing, because she is indexed as a spouse rather than a principal. Nothing further of him has been traced.

- Children (linked in tree): Suzanne Wiriot
