### Anne Philpin (c. 1630–1681)

**Place:** Metz  
**Generation:** 10  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Married Jacques Michelet, the dyer and deacon, at Metz on 30 October 1646, and died in 1681, four years before the Revocation closed the church she was married in. Hers is the correction that matters most here: many published trees give Jacques a wife “Anne Du Bois.” That comes from an Anne in the Berlin table styled *veuve Dubois* — and the table enters that Anne **as his daughter**, died 1681, not as his wife.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Jacques Michelet, m. 30 Oct 1646
- Children (linked in tree): Louis Michelet
- Children: Anne (veuve Dubois), Pierre (1652), Jacques (1661), Judith, Louis (1675)

Sources:

- [Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America (1893), “European Genealogy — La Famille Michelet de Metz, d'après des documents authentiques”: the numbered table sent from Berlin in 1883, generations 1–8](https://archive.org/stream/cu31924029842865/cu31924029842865_djvu.txt)

### Suzanne Mangeot (1674–bef. 1715)

**Place:** Metz → Zweibrücken  
**Role:** Kept the diary the family descent rests on  
**Generation:** 9  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born 26 June 1674; married Louis Michelet in 1697 at Zweibrücken, across the border in the Palatine duchy, because no Protestant marriage could be solemnised in French Metz after the Revocation. **Her own diary, still kept by the Berlin branch in 1883, records that the couple stayed at Zweibrücken until their first son was born and baptised** — the single sentence on which the whole American descent turns. She and Louis declared openly for the Reformed faith at Metz in 1699, and she was dead by 1715, when Louis declared their five surviving children Protestant as a widower.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Louis Michelet, m. 1697 at Zweibrücken
- Children (linked in tree): Jean Jacques Michelet (John Jacob Mickley)
- Children: Jean Jacques (1697), Jeanne (1699), Barbe (1702), Marie (1703), Louis (1705), Pierre (1710)
- Born: 26 Jun 1674

Sources:

- [Genealogy of the Mickley Family of America (1893), “European Genealogy — La Famille Michelet de Metz, d'après des documents authentiques”: the numbered table sent from Berlin in 1883, generations 1–8](https://archive.org/stream/cu31924029842865/cu31924029842865_djvu.txt)

### Marthe Olsdatter (c. 1820)

**Place:** Dyrnæs, Eigersund → Egersund, Rogaland, Norway  
**Role:** Spinner and washerwoman  
**Generation:** 6  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born about 1820 in the Eigersund country parish. An unmarried servant girl (Pige) at Dyrnæs when she bore twins in January 1849, naming Ole Olsen of Egersund as their father; only Olava appears in the baptismal register. She never married. In 1865 she was a worker at the spinning mill, 45, sharing a room at Storgaden 182 in the town with her daughter, a seamstress. In 1870, recorded as Martha Olsdatter Dyrnæs, she was a washerwoman living with Otto and Olava at Nyalmendingen; in 1875, at Storgade 150, the census-taker wrote that she was “supported by her son-in-law Otto Joh. Olsen.” Her granddaughter Marthe, baptized in 1874, carries her name.

- Children (linked in tree): Olava Olsdatter Olsen

### Olava Olsdatter Olsen (1849)

**Place:** Dyrnæs → Egersund, Rogaland, Norway  
**Role:** Seamstress  
**Generation:** 5  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born January 25, 1849, at Dyrnæs in the Eigersund country parish, a twin, to Marthe Olsdatter, an unmarried servant girl there, who named Ole Olsen of Egersund as the father; baptized Olave at Egersund on February 25, 1849, with the pastor's note that the child was a twin and the father named by the mother. Her twin does not appear in the register. Confirmed at Egersund on October 4, 1863, her birthplace given as Grastvedt, her knowledge graded “good,” with Ole Olsen and Marthe Olsdatter recorded as her parents. In December 1865 she was a seamstress, 18, living with her mother in a rented room at Storgaden 182 in the town. Married the cooper Otto Johan Olsen on October 18, 1866, at 18. Four children: Ole Ingeman (1867), Inger Mathilde (1868), Ingeborg Andrea (1871), and Marthe (1874), the daughter named for her mother, who lived with the family from at least 1870 until 1875. Her later life has not been traced; her daughter Inger Mathilde emigrated to Chicago.

- Parents: Ole Olsen; Marthe Olsdatter
- Spouse (linked in tree): Otto Johan Olsen
- Children (linked in tree): Inger Mathilde Olsen Christoffersen
- Parents: Ole Olsen of Egersund and Marthe Olsdatter of Dyrnæs (not married)
- Children: Ole Ingeman (1867), Inger Mathilde (1868), Ingeborg Andrea (1871), Marthe (1874)

### Adolph Martin (1851–1934)

**Place:** Baden-Württemberg → Rush Twp., Schuylkill Co., PA  
**Role:** Immigrant, 1881  
**Generation:** 4  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born April 1851 in Germany (the FamilySearch tree says April 12, in Baden-Württemberg, son of Casper and Magdalena Martin). Came to the United States in 1881 and married Catharine Herring in Schuylkill County on November 11, 1887, when two children of hers, Oscar and Emma, were already in the household. Farmed or kept house at Grier City in Rush Township, where the family appears in 1900 and 1910; of Tamaqua when he gave consent to his daughter Mary's marriage in 1911. Died June 11, 1934, at Grier City; buried at St. Peter's United Church cemetery, Barnesville. A German Protestant family.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Catharine Ardella Herring Martin
- Children (linked in tree): Mary C. Martin Millet
- Spouse: Catharine Ardella Herring, m. 11 Nov 1887

### George Baran (c. 1866)

**Place:** Hungary (Slovakia) → Bayonne, NJ  
**Role:** Immigrant, 1889  
**Generation:** 4  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born about 1865–67 in the Kingdom of Hungary, in the Slovak north that became Czechoslovakia after 1918. Came to the United States in 1889, leaving his wife Mary and their first children to follow; by 1900 the family was reunited in Bayonne, where seven more children were born. Head of a household in Bayonne's fifth ward in 1910, 1915, and 1920, when his surname was written Baron and Barren. His parish and village have not been identified; Bayonne's Slovak Catholic parish records would be the place to look.

### Julia T. Baran Nickerson (c. 1907)

**Place:** New Jersey  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born February 1907 in Bayonne, the eighth of ten children of George and Mary Baran, Slovak immigrants from what the censuses call Hungary in 1910, Austria in 1920, and Czechoslovakia in 1950; the family lived in Bayonne's fifth ward, where her three eldest siblings had been born in the old country before their father's arrival in 1889. Married Stephen Nickerson about 1924, and their first child Luella was born in Albany, New York, in June 1925, near his family's country; by 1930 they were back in Bayonne. In 1940 the household at Bayonne's third ward included her mother-in-law Orpha, by then Mrs. Kerr, and Orpha's son Thomas. A registered voter with Stephen at 100 West 33rd Street in 1942; at 830 Avenue C in 1948; in 1950 the census found them, with Thomas Kerr and an Elvira Castle, 75, at Hudson in Albany County, New York; in Dunellen and then Warrenville, New Jersey, by 1954. Still living in August 1956. Her death has not been found; the family understood her to be buried in the Dunellen–Plainfield area.

- Spouse (linked in tree): Stephen John Nickerson
- Children (linked in tree): Willard Stephen Nickerson; Luella Mae Nickerson Garman

### Louise S. Mihm Christoffersen (1890–1977)

**Place:** Jersey City → Atlantic City, NJ  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Sophie Louise Mihm, born June 1890 in Jersey City (June 19 by her birth record, June 14 by her Social Security record), eldest child of Joseph Mihm, a New York–born son of German parents, and Louisa Mayer. Called Lulu as a girl: the 1900 census has Lulu, 10, in her parents' house in the second ward with her grandfather Jacob Mayer, 70, from Germany, and three Mayer uncles and aunts, and she was Lulu again to the census-taker of 1930. Still single at 32 and living with her father in 1922, she married Walter Christoffersen, ten years younger, about 1923, and had six children between 1924 and 1937 in Jersey City's seventh ward. Widowed by April 1950, when she headed the household with Raymond and Lucille; at 52 Bergen Avenue in 1948. Died September 1977, her last address in Atlantic City; buried at Belvidere Cemetery, Warren County.

### Mary C. Martin Millet (1891–1971)

**Place:** Tamaqua, PA  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Mary Catherine Martin, born August 18, 1891, at Grier City in Rush Township, a mining patch a few miles west of Tamaqua, daughter of Adolph Martin, a German immigrant of 1881, and Catharine Herring, Pennsylvania-born. Nine in the 1900 census with six brothers and sisters; not at home in 1910, when she was 18. Her marriage licence of May 6, 1911, carries the consent of her father, Adolph Martin, then of Tamaqua, because she was under 21; she married Charles A. Millet, a store clerk of 48, at Tamaqua on May 20, 1911, before a minister of the gospel. Six children between 1913 and 1927, and a widow from 1950. Died 1971; buried at Odd Fellows Cemetery, Tamaqua. Her son Charles married Dorothy Herring, of her mother's family.

### William Earl Williams (1899–1969)

**Place:** Gilberton → Tamaqua, PA  
**Role:** Reading Railroad brake inspector  
**Generation:** 3  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born November 7, 1899, at Gilberton, a colliery town in the Mahanoy valley, son of William H. Williams, Pennsylvania-born of Welsh parents, and Cora Dornsife. Father of Marie (1922) and William (c. 1923) by a first wife whose name has not been found; by 1930 the two children were with his parents at Gilberton. Married Evelyn D. Beck, 36, at Allentown on September 20, 1930, and settled in Tamaqua's East Ward, where the family was in the same house from 1935 to 1940. Registered for the draft at Tamaqua in February 1942, employed by the Reading Railroad, five feet six, brown-eyed; a railroad brake inspector in 1950. Died April 23, 1969; buried with Evelyn in her family's plot at Highland Memorial Park, Allentown.

### Marie Violet Williams Millet (1922–2004)

**Place:** Tamaqua, PA → South Bound Brook, NJ  
**Generation:** 2  
**Status:** direct ancestor of Chris Millet

Born January 17, 1922, in Tamaqua, daughter of William Earl Williams, a Reading Railroad man from Gilberton. Her mother is not yet identified: in April 1930 Marie, 8, and her brother William, 7, were living with their grandparents William H. and Cora Williams at Gilberton, and that September her father married Evelyn Beck of Allentown, who raised the children in Tamaqua's East Ward and whom Marie's Social Security record names as her mother. Worked briefly on the assembly line at the Atlas Corporation in Tamaqua and as an usherette at the Majestic Theater, managed by her aunt; she was on shift with Fred Millet on December 7, 1941. Homemaker in South Bound Brook for 60 years; elder, deacon, and Women's Guild president at the Reformed Church of South Bound Brook; Girl Scouts and Cub Scout den mother; VFW Post 2290 auxiliary. Died December 5, 2004, at Somerset Medical Center, Somerville.

## Negative findings (searched, not found)

These are as useful as the positive results; they say where not to look again.

- **Frederick Millet's death, 1864.** No Civil War service record under Millet, Millett, or Miller; no death notice found in the Pottsville Miners' Journal for 1864 under "Millet" (the July 16, 1864 hit is an unrelated advertisement). His death is fixed only by the letters of administration of July 18, 1864.
- **Frederick Millet's baptism (1820).** Not in St. John's, Orwigsburg (register begins 1833). Zion's "Old Red" Union Church records, compiled by Philip A. Rice, are not online; John Millot's children (1811–1817) are known from that compilation via FamilySearch, which suggests Martin's children may be there too. Phase 2 confirmed that FamilySearch's indexed church records hold no Millot baptism in Schuylkill County for 1800–1830 at all, John's children included, so their absence proves nothing; the register images themselves (Ancestry's Pennsylvania church and town records, or FHL microfilm) are the next step.
- **Sgt. Frederick Millet's children.** FamilySearch's full-text index of Berks and Schuylkill deeds, probate records, and court dockets (searched September 2026) contains no Frederick Millot, Milot, Mylot, or Miloth and no document naming the parents of Maria, George, John, or Martin. The usgwarchives transcriptions of St. John's Orwigsburg were unreachable.
- **Martin Millot after 1830.** No Millot, Millet, or Millett household in Schuylkill County in the 1840 census index under any spelling; he may be indexed as Miller. No Find a Grave memorial for Martin or Catharine. The Martin Millett who bought a lot in Mahanoy City in the 1860s is an Irish-born man of a different family; the Martin Millot naturalized in Schuylkill County in 1856 is presumably the same immigrant.
- **Sgt. Frederick Millet in the censuses.** No Millot/Mylot/Milot/Millet in the Berks County entries of the 1790, 1800, or 1810 census indexes; no Millot in the Brunswick Township (Orwigsburg) tax lists of 1779–1785.
- **Frederick's origin.** No French or German baptism or immigration record found. Colebrookdale and Oley had Huguenot settlers, and Millot is common in Burgundy, Champagne, and Lorraine as well as Normandy; his origin is open.
- **FamilySearch's index of the Metz registers, searched 7 September 2026.** With an account, the collection returns **1,073 Michelet entries**. It confirms six of the table's untested claims and adds what the table left blank. Confirmed: Susanne's marriage to **Michel Persod**, 23 Jan 1600; Paul's marriage to **Anne Pillon**, 12 May 1613; Esther's death 8 Apr 1661; Suzanne's death 20 Feb 1668; Pierre son of Gédéon baptised 21 Dec 1657; and Susanne's baptism in January 1579, though the register gives **14 January against the table's 18th**. New spouses the table does not name: **Jacques (b. 1576) married Madeleine de St Blaise** on 3 May 1602; **Samuel (b. 1598) married Rachel Pilon**; **Gédéon married Madeleine de Milly**; **Suzanne (b. 1627) married Abraham Boucel**; and **Esther (b. 1603) was the wife of David de la Cloche**, which corrects the table's garbled “morte venue de David de la Cloche.” New dates: **Paul (b. 1584) died at Metz in April 1668**. And a child the table omits: a **Paul baptised 26 December 1670 to Jacques Michelet and Anne Philpin**, so that couple had more children than the six it lists.
- **Nicholas Busby's will is real, and it names William Nickerson.** Found 9 September 2026 in *Massachusetts Wills and Probate Records* under Suffolk County. The index gives the will as **dated 25 July 1657 and proved 14 October 1657**, Nicholas Busby of **Boston**, filed as **Will Papers** in three images — and it extracts as other persons named in the document **Abraham Busby** and **William Nickerson**. Savage's 1860 summary is therefore confirmed on the date to the day, on the residence, and on the presence of the son Abraham and the son-in-law William. **This is the first primary record tying the Busbys to the Nickersons**; everything before rested on Savage. **The three images have since been read** — see the Nicholas Busby entry above and Phase 3 item 15. They confirm Savage throughout, give Anne Nickerson as “my Eldest daughter”, William Nickerson and John Grout as “my sonne”, and the whole schedule of bequests, books and looms. The separate Nicholas Busby probate of 10 September 1657 under Plymouth County now looks like the witnesses' deposition to this same will, which the register dates to that day.
- **Nicklauß Möloth signed the Lydia's list in his own hand.** The candidate father is not an OCR ghost. Strassburger's facsimile volume reproduces the signatures for **Ship Lydia, 9 October 1749, List 142 C**, and on p. 480 of that volume, in the left column, stands the autograph **“Nicklauß Möloth.”** Three things follow. He was **literate** — he signed rather than making a mark. The date is **9 October**, not the 13th that Egle's OCR gave, so that discrepancy is closed. And he wrote the name with an **umlaut**, *Möloth*, which is a German orthography rather than a French *Millot* — consistent with a French family already Germanised in the Palatinate, and the obvious ancestor of the *Miloth* spelling Sgt. Frederick used on the 1782 muster roll. The name still cannot be joined to Frederick by any document.

  Strassburger's **first** volume, the printed transcription, was read on 9 September 2026 and adds four things. It prints the name as **“Nicklaus Möloth”**, umlaut and all, so the editors read it as I did off the facsimile — and it prints it **without a mark-cross**, where a good many of his neighbours on that page carry an `(X)` or an `(O)` for men who could not write. His literacy is therefore attested twice over, in the autograph and in the transcription's own notation. Second, the list gives the Lydia's people as coming from **“Wirtemberg, Dourlach, Zweybrecht, Palatinate”** — Württemberg, Durlach, **Zweibrücken** and the Palatinate — and Zweibrücken, a French-speaking-adjacent duchy on the Lorraine border, is where a French surname would most plausibly already have been Germanised into *Möloth*. Third, and against the lead as it was framed, **only the C list survives for this ship**. Strassburger prints lists A (the captain's list, with ages), B and C where they exist; for the Lydia of 9 October 1749 there is no A and no B. **His age cannot be recovered from the passenger records at all**, so the father-or-grandfather question has to be settled in Berks County, not in Philadelphia's arrival papers. Fourth, a whole-volume sweep for every spelling — Möloth, Moloth, Miloth, Milot, Mylot, Mellot, Malot, Millot — returns **exactly one hit in the entire work**. Strassburger covers every German-speaking male of sixteen or over landed at Philadelphia between 1727 and 1775, about three hundred ships; in half a century no other family of this name came through that port. The bound on that negative is worth stating: it excludes women, children, British subjects, and every port that was not Philadelphia.
- **The Pike congregation is St. Joseph's (Hill) Lutheran Church.** Identified 9 September 2026 from the record detail: Lutheran, Pike Township, Berks County, the records held by the Historical Society of Pennsylvania. **Hill Church was already named in these notes**, as one of the registers in F. Edward Wright's *Berks County Church Records of the 18th Century* — so the two leads are the same lead, and the Millots are now documented members of that congregation. Ancestry's index of it holds **no Millot baptisms**, only the 1805 confirmations and communicant list, so the register itself or Wright's transcription is the next step.
- **The Pike congregation kept more than one list, and Millots were still at Oley in the 1850s.** Searched 9 September 2026. Besides the confirmations of 9 June 1805, the same source carries Martin, John and Eva a month later, on **9 July 1805**, which reads like a communicant roll — so the congregation kept running membership records, not a one-off. Three later Millot entries sit at **Oley**: Sarah Ann (28 September 1851), Sarah (1854) and Susana (5 November 1854), consistent with George Milot and Maria Milot Liess having stayed in the Oley valley. The congregation at Pike has **not** yet been identified by name, and that identification is now the most valuable single step on this line: a church keeping confirmation and communicant lists in 1805 will have kept baptisms through the 1780s and 1790s, and a baptism is the one document that would name Sgt. Frederick as a father.
- **Martin, John and Eva Millot confirmed together at Pike, Berks County, 9 June 1805.** Found on Ancestry, 7 September 2026, in *Pennsylvania and New Jersey Church and Town Records*. This is the first record placing Martin and John in Berks County at all — until now they appeared only at Orwigsburg from 1815, and their Berks origin was inferred from the rarity of the surname. Pike Township adjoins the Oley and Colebrookdale district where Sgt. Frederick Millet was taxed in 1781, so the geography now supports what was only an inference. **Eva Millot is a fourth sibling not previously recorded here.** Confirmation at fourteen to sixteen puts their births about 1789–91, which is later than the 1777–85 range this file assigns Sgt. Frederick's probable children and needs reconciling. The index extracts no parents for any of the three.
- **Where the proof of Martin → Frederick is not.** Three places were checked on Ancestry and none holds it. There is **no baptism for a Frederick Millet or Millot about 1820** anywhere in *Pennsylvania and New Jersey Church and Town Records*, 14.2 million records — the collection open lead 6 pointed at, now eliminated. There is **no estate for Martin Millot anywhere in Pennsylvania probate**, searched across all counties, so no document names his heirs. And the index entry for Frederick's own letters of administration of 18 July 1864 lists **only Frederick**, extracting neither the administratrix nor the sureties. The link therefore still rests where it did: a boy aged 10–14 in Martin's household in the 1830 census, and the FamilySearch tree.
- **Frederick Millet in the 1863 federal tax lists.** *U.S., IRS Tax Assessment Lists, 1862–1918* carries two entries for a Frederick Millet resident in Pennsylvania in 1863, the year before his death. Unexamined; a record type not previously consulted for him, and one that would give an occupation and a valuation.
- **The three Michelet wives all have fathers in the register.** Searched 7 September 2026. Anne Philpin's father was **Pierre Philpin**; Suzanne Wiriot's was **Jean Wiriot**; and Marie Colin's was **Daniel Colin**, which confirms the Berlin table's *Daniel Collin, praticien du palais* from the record itself. The table names only the last of the three, so two of these are new. The Philpin marriage entry also indexes **seven children** of Jacques Michelet and Anne Philpin — Paul, Judith, Jacques, Pierre, Louis, Anne, and a **David** the table never mentions — against the five the table lists.
- **A search can miss what the record holds.** Suzanne Wiriot's father is on the 1591 marriage record, but a surname search for Wiriot in the same collection returns nothing at all: she is indexed as a spouse, not a principal, so the field is invisible to that query. The lesson for this line is to open the record page rather than trust a name search, and it is the second time the index has hidden something it contains.
- **No Michelet marriage at Metz before 1576 is indexed.** Searched 7 September 2026 across 1550–1580: the only marriage returned in range is a Michel at Sedan, a different family. Jacquemin's first marriage, to Suzanne Joly, therefore remains unevidenced outside the Berlin table — the register run may begin too late, or the entry may simply be unindexed.
- **The FamilySearch index has gaps, so absence there proves nothing.** A search on the given name Samuel returns no Michelet at all, although the same collection demonstrably holds the baptism of 29 March 1634 to Samuel Michelet and Rachel Pilon. The failure to find the baptisms of Paul 1584, Pierre 1582, Samuel 1598 and Esther 1603 is therefore **not** evidence against them; those four are untested rather than disproved.
- **Ancestry is the wrong tool for this line; FamilySearch is decisively better.** Checked 7 September 2026 at Chris's suggestion. Ancestry's only Moselle collection is *Moselle, France, Births, Marriages and Deaths, **1793**–1922* — it begins a hundred and eight years after the Metz Protestant registers end in 1685, and Ancestry holds no ancien régime parish or Protestant registers for Metz at all. A direct search for Anne Philpin returns hits in Eure-et-Loir, Brussels, London, Wales, Cher and the German Lutheran collections, and none at Metz. FamilySearch, by contrast, indexes the Metz Reformed registers in *France, Protestant Church Records 1536–1902*, which is where every Metz record in this file comes from. Ancestry earns its place elsewhere in the tree — the American and Norwegian material — but not here.
- **Ancestry does not hold the Berlin French Colony registers.** Its Berlin and Prussian church collections are Lutheran — *Berlin and Brandenburg, selected Lutheran … 1758–1874*, *Kingdom of Prussia … Lutheran … 1661–1944*, *Brandenburg, Transcripts of Church Records, 1700–1874* — with civil registration only from 1874. The Huguenot colony kept its own registers and they are not there. Worse for the lead, Ancestry's Michelet hits for 1705 are the 1893 Mickley genealogy recirculating as *Family History Books* and *Public Member Stories*: the same source coming back round, not a second witness.
- **Three more Metz entries, and a second date conflict.** A Pierre Michelet, son of Gédéon, died at Metz on 21 March 1651; a Marie Michelet, daughter of Jean, died there in 1580; and one entry is indexed as the baptism of “Jacquermin Michelet” at Metz in 1576, probably the 14 October baptism of Jacques indexed under his father's name. Against the table's Daniel (b. 1615), *morte 21 Juin 1659*, the register gives a Dan. Michelet dying at Metz on **21 February 1659** — the same day of the month, a different month, which reads as a misreading rather than a different man.
- **Two patterns the table hides.** Two of Jacquemin's sons married two Pilons — Paul to Anne in 1613, Samuel to Rachel — and the Michelets married the de la Cloches twice in successive generations: Esther Michelet was David de la Cloche's wife, and her nephew Daniel married David's daughter Judith in 1645.
- **The Metz Protestant registers are online after all.** The archive's classification lists *Registres des Protestants (1561–1685)* without children, which reads as undigitised; the items are in fact there, under two sub-series, **Protestants Metz [18]** and **Protestants Pays messin [5]**. The Metz eighteen are **GG236–GG253**, about 6,500 views: naissances-mariages 1561–1667 (GG236–GG244, GG246–GG247), décès 1625–1667 (GG245), and naissances-mariages-décès 1667–1685 (GG248–GG253). They are chronological and carry no index, so a dated claim is reached by navigating to the year — GG239 covers 1581–1585, with February 1585 at view 157 and April 1585 at view 165. The hand is period secretary script and hard to read from a screen.
- **The Berlin table's first generation is not a generation.** The eight people the 1893 book prints as the children of Jehan Michelet span 1471 to 1610. They cannot be siblings, so the Michelet line above Jacquemin is a collection of notable bearers of the name, not a descent. Karl Michelet warned that anything before 1444 was conjectural; this shows the caution needs extending forward to about 1550.
- **GG224 read: no Michelet marriage in the Sainte Croix section.** The marriage table GG224 (Mariages 1566–1729, I–Z) was opened in the archive's viewer on 7 September 2026 and is legible. It is arranged **by parish section, then alphabetically by the groom's surname**, each entry giving groom and his parents, bride and hers, the date, and the parish. In the **Sainte Croix** section the run reads MICHAUD (1696), then seven MICHEL marriages between 1655 and 1695, and the next page opens at MONTREUX and MORARD: **the sequence passes straight from MICHEL to MO- with no MICHELET**. That clears one parish of fifteen. The volume runs to 779 views and the section boundaries are not catalogued, so a complete check means finding the M pages inside each parish section in turn — St Victor's L pages sit at view 190, Sainte Croix's M at 198, its P at 400. Views 191 and similar are blank dividers.
- **No baptism index exists at Metz for 1702–1710.** The Archives municipales de Metz catalogue was read directly on 7 September 2026. Its classification confirms the point deduced from FamilySearch: the *Registres des Protestants* run **1561–1685** and stop at the Revocation, while the Catholic *Registres des paroisses et des hôpitaux* run **1566–1793** and are online for fifteen Metz parishes — Sainte Croix, Saint Etienne, Saint Eucaire, Saint Gengoulf, Saint George, Saint Gorgon, Saint Jean, Saint Livier, Saint Marcel, Saint Martin, Saint Maximin, Sainte Ségolène, Saint Simon, Saint Simplice, Saint Victor — plus five hospitals. But the *ancien régime* decennial tables cover **marriages only**: GG223 (Mariages 1566–1729, A–H) and GG224 (Mariages 1566–1729, I–Z). Birth tables begin only with GG225, in 1730. There is therefore no name index to the baptisms of 1702–1710, and no way to find the four children except by reading the registers of the fifteen parishes for those years, without knowing which parish the family belonged to.
- **The Metz baptisms of 1702–1710 are not in the FamilySearch Protestant collection.** Checked 7 September 2026. Individual FamilySearch record pages (`ark:` URLs) are readable without an account, but the search interface is behind a sign-in, so the collection could not be searched from here. It would not have helped: “France, Protestant Church Records, 1536–1902” (collection 1582585) is a Protestant collection whose Metz registers end with the Revocation of 1685, which is why every Metz record already cited here falls between 1576 and 1685. The four certificates Karl Michelet described are Catholic or civil records, and Metz publishes its own registers rather than leaving them to the Moselle departmental archives.
- **Zweibrücken, searched in person in 1869.** The 1893 genealogy prints a paper of 1863 whose author visited Zweibrücken in 1869 and had the records examined at his request: he was told that nothing could be found to show that such a family had ever resided in that country, and that the record, if it had existed, was lost. This is a stronger negative than an absent index entry, and it is eighty years older than any online catalogue. No indexed baptism for Jean Jacques Michelet (1697). The church books are held by the Zentralarchiv der Evangelischen Kirche der Pfalz in Speyer and are partly on Archion; the 1883 letter from Berlin says the Protestant records of the period were probably lost.
- **Suzanne Mangeot.** Not found in the Metz Protestant registers (which end in 1685; she was born in 1674 and the family's declarations of 1699 and 1715 are in the civil archives cited by the Berlin table, not in the church books).
- **Thomas Moyer's marriage, 1833.** No indexed marriage record on FamilySearch for Thomas Moyer and Susanna Mickley; the date of 6 August 1833 in South Whitehall stands on the FamilySearch tree without an attached source. No 1869 death notice for him in GenealogyBank's Pennsylvania papers. An 1840 census entry indexed "Thomas Moyer, amaly" is Amity Township, Berks County, not Tamaqua, and is not him.
- **Marie Violet Williams's mother.** No record names her. William Earl Williams's first marriage (c. 1920–21) and the mother's death or departure before April 1930 have not been searched beyond the census; Evelyn Beck's identification as stepmother rests on her being single in the April 1930 census and marrying in September 1930.
- **Julia Baran Nickerson's death and marriage.** No SSDI/NUMIDENT death entry for a Julia Nickerson born 1907; no New Jersey marriage index entry for Nickerson–Baran (FamilySearch's New Jersey coverage does not include the mid-1920s). Her first child was born in Albany, New York, in June 1925, so an Albany County marriage of 1924–25 is likely. Her exact birth date is known only as February 1907. No Find a Grave memorial found.
- **Louise Mihm's marriage and Walter Christoffersen's death.** No indexed New Jersey marriage for Christoffersen–Mihm (c. 1923); no death record found for Walter, who is absent from the 1948 engagement notice and whose widow calls herself widowed in April 1950. No evidence of an earlier marriage for Louise: she was still Mihm, at her father's address, in the 1922 directory.
- **Martin Christoffersen's Danish parish.** Not found; the Danish emigration archives (udvandrerarkivet.dk) could not be reached from the research sessions and remain the best lead.
- **Willard Nickerson's exact birth date.** July 1927 by a residence record, 1928 by the censuses.
- **Jersey Journal "Nickerson" and "Christoffersen" hits of 1936–1937.** All belong to other families in the Lafayette section of Jersey City (Gertrude and Audrey Nickerson).
- **Sgt. Frederick Millet in Savage.** Savage's *Genealogical Dictionary of the First Settlers of New England* covers only arrivals before May 1693 and only New England, so it cannot reach a Pennsylvania family of the 1780s. All four volumes were searched anyway in September 2026. Volume II carries a **Thomas Millet of Gloucester, Massachusetts**, whose wife Mary came in 1635 on the *Elizabeth*: an English Puritan family with no connection to this one, which wrote itself Millot, Mylot and Miloth in nearly every record before the 1840s — with the 1767 warrant application and the 1780 pay roll the known exceptions, both of which write *Millet*. Recorded here so the coincidence of surname is not mistaken for a lead a second time.
- **Millot in the 1790 census of the whole state.** The 1790 Pennsylvania volume was searched end to end, not only its Berks entries: no Millot of any spelling in Berks, confirming the earlier finding. The only cluster in the state is in **Bedford County** — Jacob, Dory, Benjamin, and John Millott, with Joseph Melot nearby — unexamined, and probably an unrelated English family.
- **Olava Olsdatter's twin.** The 1849 baptism calls her a twin, but no second baptism or burial for a twin appears in the Egersund register.

## The Jean-François Millet question

Family lore links the Millets to the painter Jean-François Millet (1814–1875). There is no evidence for it and the timeline argues against it. The painter was born at Gruchy, Gréville-Hague, Normandy, to Jean-Louis-Nicolas Millet (1791–1835); his grandfather was Marin Nicolas Millet (1750–1813), and the family's tree runs back through Norman farmers to a Jean Millet born in 1706, all in La Hague. Chris's line was already in Pennsylvania before the painter's father was born, and wrote itself Millot, Mylot and Miloth in nearly every record before the 1840s. **The spelling argument is dead.** The printed *Pennsylvania Archives* gives the sergeant as *Frederick Millet* in 1780, and that could have been dismissed as an editor's reading of 1906 — but the Land Office warrant application of **27 July 1767** is a manuscript, and the clerk wrote *Frederick Millet* there too. The modern spelling is in the record from the very first appearance of the family in Pennsylvania. The timeline and the geography still argue against the painter on their own: any connection would have to lie in France before about 1750, where the Pennsylvania family's origin is unknown. Sources: Britannica; Geneastar family tree of Jean-François Millet; Musée d'Orsay.

## Open leads, in rough order of promise

1. ~~**Read the three images of Nicholas Busby's will.**~~ **Done, 9 September 2026** — Suffolk County probate register, volume pp. 308–311, images saved under `assets/`. The wording, the three daughters, the bequests, the books and the two looms are all transcribed in the Nicholas Busby entry above, and Anne is now fixed as “my Eldest daughter” and William Nickerson as “my sonne” from the father's own side. What remains from this thread is small: the **Plymouth County probate of 10 September 1657**, which the register's own dating suggests is really the witnesses' deposition to this same will rather than a second filing, and Bridget's surname, which the will does not give — she is only “my deare & loving wife”. Bigger, and newly opened by it: **Busby's Norwich origins**, since a weaver who owned Perkins, Willet, Cogan and Barrough and three Bibles is a man likely to appear in Norwich's own records, and the will names two witnesses, **Nathaniell Woodward** and **Wm Pearse**, who are traceable Boston men.
2. **Salem or Boston, and 8 or 15 April 1637.** Savage and the Nickerson Family Association disagree on both the port of arrival and the sailing date. The passenger list of the *Rose* or the *John and Dorothy*, or Winthrop's journal for June 1637, should settle it.
3. **Identify the Pike Township congregation, then read its baptisms of the 1780s–90s.** The confirmations of Martin, John and Eva on 9 June 1805, and the communicant list of 9 July, come from a named church in Ancestry's *Pennsylvania and New Jersey Church and Town Records*; opening the record detail gives the congregation. Pike adjoins Colebrookdale, where Sgt. Frederick was taxed in 1781. If that register runs back twenty years it should hold the baptisms of the three of them, and a baptism entry names the father. **This is the likeliest route to proving or breaking Sgt. Frederick's paternity**, and it also bears on the age problem: confirmation at fourteen to sixteen puts these births about 1789–91, a decade later than the 1777–85 range assumed for his children. If that range is right and the confirmations are not late, Martin and John may be his **grandchildren** rather than his children, and a generation is missing from this line.
4. **Nicholas Miloth, ship Lydia, 1749 — Sgt. Frederick's likely father.** Egle's oath-of-allegiance lists hold one `Nickolaus Miiloth` (OCR), last of 108 names on the Lydia, Capt. John Randolph, from Rotterdam, qualified 13 October 1749 — the only name of the kind in the volume, and matching Frederick's own spelling in the 1782 muster. An oath-taker was sixteen or older, so a birth by 1733, which fits a father for a man taxed as a tailor in 1781. To settle it: **Strassburger's *Pennsylvania German Pioneers* vol. II**, which reproduces the signatures for this list, and then **Berks County estate and Orphans' Court records for a Nicholas Miloth, c. 1750–1785**. Not entered in the tree until the signature is read. **The signature has now been read** (Strassburger vol. II, p. 480): he wrote *Nicklauß Möloth*, in his own hand, and the ship qualified on 9 October. ~~and the captain's list for List 142 in Strassburger vol. I, which would give his age~~ — **that route is now closed**: vol. I was read on 9 September 2026 and only the C list survives for this ship, there being no captain's list and no B list, so no age exists to be found. The same reading strengthened him in three other ways: the printed transcription gives “Nicklaus Möloth” with the umlaut and without a mark-cross, confirming both spelling and literacy; the Lydia's people came from Württemberg, Durlach, **Zweibrücken** and the Palatinate; and his is the **only** name of this kind in the whole of Strassburger, 1727–1775. What remains is therefore entirely on this side of the Atlantic: **Berks or Philadelphia County estate and Orphans' Court records for a Möloth, Moloth or Miloth between 1750 and 1785**, which is now the only way to settle whether he is Frederick's father or his grandfather. Three German-side routes were tried on 9 September 2026 and all came back empty — the Zweibrücken manumission list, the LEO-BW emigration database for Baden and Württemberg, and the Goshenhoppen Catholic registers on this side. See Phase 3 item 17 for how far each of those negatives actually reaches, which is not as far as it sounds.
5. **The Berlin French Colony registers.** Four of this family went to Berlin — Pierre the colonel des milices in 1685, Suzanne with her husband Quien, Louis in 1720, and Marie who married Pierre Perrin there — and the colony kept its own registers, courts, and rolls from 1672, separate from the Prussian parish system. This is the **one archive that could corroborate the Metz table independently**, which matters more than any single fact in it: everything above Jean Jacques currently rests on one document written in 1883. A Berlin entry for Louis, arriving 1720 as the son of Louis Michelet and Suzanne Mangeot of Metz, would put the table on two legs instead of one. **Checked 7 September 2026: Ancestry does not hold these.** Its Berlin and Prussian church collections are Lutheran and its civil registration starts in 1874; the colony's own registers are on no subscription site reached so far. This lead has to go to the **Archiv der Französischen Kirche zu Berlin**, by correspondence rather than search.
6. **Finish testing the table against the Metz Protestant registers — nine of its claims already hold.** Scored on 7 September 2026 against the register entries this project had already opened, the table is right every time it can be checked: the marriage of 28 July 1591, the baptisms of 14 October 1576, 20 September 1592, 7 April 1619 and 17 December 1675, the marriages of 8 February 1615, 30 April 1645 and 30 October 1646, and the burial of 8 October 1685 — nine entries, each to the exact day, plus Paul's wife confirmed as Anne Pilon. **One discrepancy**: the table gives a Jacques, son of Jacques and Anne Philpin, baptised 16 February 1661, where the register has a Jacques of the same parents baptised 10 June 1657. Either two sons of the name, the elder having died, or a slip in the table. The registers are online — 18 volumes, GG236–GG253, listed under Negative findings — and are chronological, so any dated claim can be reached directly. The obstacle is reading: 1580s secretary hand at the limit of what a screenshot supports. **The efficient route is FamilySearch**, which has already indexed these same registers (every Metz record cited here came from it) and whose search needs an account. About a dozen claims remain untested, chiefly the baptisms of Susanne 1579, Pierre 1582, Paul 1584, Samuel 1598, Esther 1603, Paul 1617, Esther 1619, Jacques 1623 and Suzanne 1627, and the marriage of Jacquemin to Suzanne Joly before 1576.
7. **The Girard marriage the table does not show.** Louis (1705) went to Berlin with David Girard, whom the table calls *le neveu de son père* — his father's nephew, so a Michelet cousin. The Girards then recur twice more: Anne Michelet, born 1682, married a David Girard, and Karl Ludwig Michelet's mother was Wilhelmine Victoire Girard. The Berlin silk firm was Girard, Michelet et Compagnie for three generations. A Girard–Michelet marriage a generation before 1720 is implied and missing; finding it would tie the Berlin and Metz branches together properly.
8. **The Metz offices.** Jacquemin was *receveur* in 1587 and *commis du trésorier du Roy* by 1593; Josué Pillon was *receveur des deniers de la bullette*, a municipal officer of the wine duty; Daniel Collin was a *praticien du palais*. Office-holders leave municipal accounts and parlement rolls where ordinary tradesmen leave nothing. The Metz archives' *liasses anciennes* and *Fonds et Versements* are where to look, and this is the only route that would put flesh on the family's standing rather than just its dates.
9. **Falsify the top of the table.** It claims an Antoine Jacques Michelet, seigneur d'Interville, as *président au parlement de Rouen* in 1596. That is a public office with a published prosopography — Frondeville, *Les présidents du Parlement de Normandie, 1499–1790* — so the claim can be confirmed or destroyed cheaply. Given that the same first generation spans 1471 to 1610 and cannot be a sibling group, knowing whether its individual claims are true or invented would tell us how much weight the rest of the table can carry.
10. **The Metz baptisms of Barbe, Marie, Louis, and Pierre, 1702–1710.** Karl Michelet says Metz holds certificates for the four younger children and none for the eldest. Reading them would confirm the family group and fix the parents' residence year by year, though it can no longer decide the American descent: the eldest's absence is explained by the family's move to Metz about 1699. The registers are online at the **Archives municipales de Metz** (archives.metz.fr → Registres numérisés → *Registres des paroisses et des hôpitaux, 1566–1793*), which publishes the city's registers itself. There is **no index for these years** — the *ancien régime* tables cover marriages only, GG223 and GG224 — so it means reading fifteen parishes' registers for 1702–1710 blind, since the family's parish is unknown. GG224 has now been partly read: the Sainte Croix section holds no Michelet marriage, so fourteen parish sections remain, each needing its own M pages found by eye. The other cheap approach is untried — the city's statistical archives hold the declarations of 1699 and 1715 that Karl quotes, which may name a parish outright.
11. **Marie Violet Williams's birth mother.** A Schuylkill County marriage of William Earl Williams about 1920–21, and a death or divorce before April 1930; then the 1920 census of Gilberton or Tamaqua for the couple. This is the sharpest remaining gap close to the present.
12. **The Zion (Red Church) register images, 1818–1822**, for Frederick Millet's baptism and Martin Millot's family; Ancestry's "Pennsylvania and New Jersey Church and Town Records" or FHL film. The single record that would prove or break Martin → Frederick. **Ancestry checked 7 September 2026 and does not have it**: no Frederick Millet or Millot baptism of about 1820 appears anywhere in *Pennsylvania and New Jersey Church and Town Records*. That leaves the FHL microfilm of the Rice compilation, or the register itself, as the only route.
13. **St. John's Orwigsburg confirmation lists of the mid-1830s** (usgwarchives transcription, or the DAR register on FamilySearch), for a Frederick Millet confirmed at 14–16 with his parents' names.
14. **F. Edward Wright's *Berks County Church Records of the 18th Century*** (Oley Reformed, Hill Church, Colebrookdale-area registers) for baptisms of Maria (1777) and George (1782) Milot naming Frederick as father.
15. **Schuylkill County Orphans' Court dockets, 1830s–1840s**, for a guardianship of Martin's minor children if he died before 1841; **Berks County estate and Orphans' Court records** for a Frederick Millot/Mylot, c. 1785–1810.
16. **Julia Baran Nickerson**: an Albany County, New York, marriage of 1924–25; her death (after August 1956) and burial in the Dunellen–Plainfield area; George Baran's village, through Bayonne's Slovak Catholic parish records and the 1889 passenger lists.
17. **Louise Mihm and Walter Christoffersen**: their marriage (Hudson County, c. 1923) and Walter's death (between 1940 and 1950); the Jersey Journal death notice of 9 June 1949 for a "Miam" that names Louise S. Mihm and a sister Irene A. Rehbein, probably a parent's death.
18. **Mary Catherine Martin's parents**: Adolph Martin's village in Baden-Württemberg (his 1881 arrival and any naturalization), and Catharine Herring's parents and first marriage, since Oscar and Emma were born before 1887.
19. **The Moyer line above Jacob**: verify Peter Meyer Sr. and Catharina Kern from the Egypt Reformed and Jordan Lutheran registers, and Martin Meyer Sr. above them; and settle whether the Daniel Moyer appointed guardian in 1818 is the Daniel Moyer who married Mary Magdalena Mickley, which would connect the Moyers and Mickleys twice.
20. **The Mickley wives** — Elizabeth Barbara Burkhalter, Susanne Margarethe Miller, Maria Salome Biery — and **the Cape Cod wives** — Mercy Williams, Deliverance Lombard, Dorcas Covel — all of whom belong to documented families and appear only in the facts fields.
21. **Egersund**: which of the Ole Olsens of the town was Olava's father; the parents of Ole Andreas Olsen and Inger Marie Thorsdatter; the marriages of both couples. Digitalarkivet handles all of these.
22. **Danish emigration records (1885)** for Martin Christoffersen's home parish.
23. **The Pottsville Mining Register of 11 February 1860 and Weekly Miners' Journal of 25 December 1874**, both of which mention Millot and Orwigsburg (GenealogyBank), unread.
24. **Zweibrücken church books on Archion** for a 1697 baptism of Jean Jacques Michelet.
25. **The Jersey Journal of 24 June 1943 (Willard Nickerson) and the Newark Star-Ledger of 30 January 1977 and 15 April 1999 (Willard Nickerson)**, unread.
26. **The PHMC Revolutionary War Military Abstract Card File** (RG-13, series 13.50, 145 boxes). Transcriptions from the original Pennsylvania militia records, giving name and rank, **active or inactive duty, county of residence, battalion, the record the entry was drawn from, and whether the man was delinquent and fined or had his fines abated**. Frederick's two known services come from the printed *Pennsylvania Archives*; this file is the underlying card index and may show more services, or fines, which would pin his residence year by year through the war. The old ARIAS site at `digitalarchives.state.pa.us` is **dead**; the file has moved to the Pennsylvania State Archives' Eloquent catalogue at `gencat.eloquent-systems.com/pennsylvania-state-archives/permalink.html?key=coll9903`, whose search resisted automation. It is also on **FamilySearch** and **Findmypast**. Alongside it, the **Militia Officers Index Cards, 1775–1800** (`key=coll9891`), and the **Militia Loan of 1784–85** certificate numbers on microfilm roll 4497, which would place him after the war. **This is the best next action on Sgt. Frederick.**
27. **Follow the Longswamp land — two of the three routes now closed.** ~~the Longswamp church registers~~ — the **Reformed** book has been read in full and holds no Millet (Phase 3 item 17); the **Lutheran** book of the same union church has *not*, is not freely transcribed, and is now the single most promising unread document for this family. ~~the Berks County deed~~ — Berks deeds of 1767–1794 are **not in FamilySearch's full-text corpus** at all, so this needs browsable films or the county Recorder of Deeds; a deed of that period usually names a wife and sometimes says how the grantor came by the tract, which is where a father would surface. Still untried and cheap: **the warrant or patent in Frederick's own name** in the Land Office series (Warrants 1766 and the *Pennsylvania Patent Records 1764–1968* are both in the corpus), which would date his acquisition and may name a prior owner; and his neighbours **Nicholas Harm(e)** and **Jacob Weiss**, who are traceable and would fix the tract on the ground.
28. **Pennsylvania Archives, Fifth and Sixth Series** — the muster rolls themselves, behind the card file. Sixth Series vol. 1, *Muster rolls relating to the associators and militia*, is on archive.org as `pennsylvaniaarch0001unse_k5e9`. Reading Capt. Strouch's and Capt. Keefer's rolls in full would give his company mates, and the Berks militia fine books would give the defaulters, which is often where a man's township is stated outright.
29. **Burgert, *Eighteenth Century Emigrants from German-Speaking Lands to North America*, vol. II: The Western Palatinate** (Pennsylvania German Society, 1985) — 519 emigrants with home villages, and it incorporates the Zweibrücken manumission protocol tested above. Not free; a library or interlibrary loan item. **Werner Hacker's *Auswanderungen aus Rheinpfalz und Saarland im 18. Jahrhundert*** is the much larger companion and would be the serious test of the German origin, since it is not limited to those who left with permission.
30. **FamilySearch tree housekeeping**: Julia Baran exists twice (LY43-KRL and G6NQ-Y6R) and Louise Mihm twice (LB74-WK6 and 9Q29-BF9, the latter as Sophie L.); Thomas Moyer's death and burial have no attached source there.
## Method and tools

Records were located through FamilySearch (indexed records, the shared tree, full-text search of deed, probate, and court books, record images, and the Digital Library), the Norwegian Digitalarkivet (transcribed parish registers and the 1865, 1870, and 1875 censuses), Find a Grave, archive.org (the 1893 Mickley genealogy and Pennsylvania Archives volumes), GenealogyBank (Berks and Schuylkill Journal, Miners' Journal, Jersey Journal), the Nickerson Family Association's website, and published references (Wikipedia, Britannica, the Historical Society of Pennsylvania, the American Numismatic Society). Newspaper pages were read from PDF clips and OCR; deed and docket transcriptions come from FamilySearch's full-text search and were checked against the page images. Where a fact came from a search-result summary or a FamilySearch tree profile rather than an opened record, the source line says so.

## Phase 2 log (September 6–7, 2026)

1. **Nickerson Family Association history.** Read the Association's history page and its article series. The 2013 "Come to Town" article and Parts II–III of the 2017 series were added as sources for William Nickerson, Anne Busby, William Jr., Ensign William, and James; Nicholas and Bridget Busby were added as Anne's parents. Discrepancies noted and left unresolved: the article gives Anne as about five years younger than William (c. 1608–09 against the tree's 1607) and both deaths as "late 1680s" against 1686 and 1690.
2. **The unproved links.** Ole Olsen → Olava: proved (nine Digitalarkivet records; the baptism, confirmation, and 1875 census read directly). Marthe Olsdatter added as Olava's mother; the two never married; the children of Otto and Olava corrected to four, with Ole Ingeman (1867) added. Sgt. Frederick → Martin/John/George/Maria and Martin → Frederick: still unproved after full-text searches of the Berks and Schuylkill deed, probate, and docket collections; the entries now say what was checked.
3. **The Moyer line.** Thomas Moyer's baptism (Egypt Reformed, 1805), the 1818 Orphans' Court guardianship (read from the page image), the 1850 and 1860 censuses (page images read for occupation: potter, high constable), and the nine children from the censuses and the FamilySearch tree. Jacob Meyer, Christina Newan, Peter Meyer Sr., Catharina Kern, and Martin Meyer Sr. (probable) added; Cpl. Zachary Taylor Moyer added as a sibling of Catharine.
4. **The direct-line women.** Mary Catherine Martin (delayed birth record, 1900 and 1910 censuses, 1911 marriage register, parents Adolph Martin and Catharine Herring added); Marie Violet Williams (NUMIDENT, 1930 Gilberton census, 1930 Beck marriage, 1940 and 1950 censuses, 1942 draft card; William Earl Williams, Evelyn Beck as stepmother, William H. Williams, and Cora Dornsife added); Julia Baran (1910, 1915, 1920, 1940, and 1950 censuses, daughter's NUMIDENT; George and Mary Baran added); Louise Mihm (1900 census, 1922 directory, SSDI, 1950 census, Find a Grave; Joseph Mihm, Louisa Mayer, and Jacob Mayer added). The Beck marriage, the Martin delayed birth record, and the 1900 Mihm census were opened and checked directly; the rest were opened by the research agents and reported with their exact wording.
5. **Publication.** The interactive tree was republished after each step. The GitHub repository (chrismilletpsu/milletfamilytree) could not be pushed from the research session because the repository was not attached to it; the updated `index.html` and this document are delivered as files for a local commit.


## Phase 3 log (September 7, 2026)

1. **Sgt. Frederick Millet, deeper search.** *Pennsylvania Archives* Third Series vol. XVIII was read in full: it holds exactly one Millot of any spelling, `Mylot, Fred'k, taylor,` under Colebrookdale, Berks County, 1781, with no valuation against his name — a landless artisan. The 1790 census was then searched for the whole of Pennsylvania rather than Berks alone, confirming his absence and turning up an unrelated-looking Millott cluster in Bedford County. Egle's oath-of-allegiance lists yielded the one candidate above him: **Nickolaus Miiloth**, last of 108 names on the ship Lydia, Capt. John Randolph, from Rotterdam, qualified 13 October 1749. The spelling matches Frederick's own in the 1782 muster. It rests on a single OCR pass of a single scan, could not be corroborated in a second scan or in Strassburger, and is **not** entered in the tree; see Open leads.
2. **Savage's *Genealogical Dictionary*.** All four volumes searched. Volume I gave Nicholas Busby's age, trade, offices, death, and the will of 25 July 1657 naming six children and a grandson — filling what had been the largest gap on that side. Volume III gave William Nickerson's age and trade, named the four children who crossed in 1637, and put the landing at Boston on 20 June rather than Salem, with embarkation on 8 April rather than 15. Volume II supplied the Eldred, Hedges, and Grout cross-references. Volume IV yielded nothing usable: Mercy Williams is unfindable among the Williamses, and Dorcas Covel (b. 1714) falls past Savage's 1693 cutoff.
3. **People added.** Nicholas Busby Jr., John Busby, Abraham Busby, Catharine Busby Savory, and Sarah Busby Grout as Anne's siblings; Elizabeth Nickerson Eldred and Ann Nickerson Hedges as her daughters. Six of the seven crossed in 1637 and carry the event marker in the tree.
4. **The Michelet line below Louis.** The 1893 genealogy was read directly rather than cited at second hand. Karl Ludwig Michelet's letters of April and July 1883 show that the descent from Louis Michelet to the Pennsylvania immigrant is an argument from elimination made after he was told what the Americans believed, and that he had marked the name Jean with a question mark in his own table beforehand; the 1863 paper records that the Zweibrücken registers were searched in person in 1869 and yielded nothing. The link is now marked probable in the tree, and both entries say what it rests on. The same reading turned up the Metz baptism dates for the four younger children, and two date conflicts in the direct line: the genealogy has Peter as 1772–1861 against the 1773–1863 taken from Find a Grave, and Susanna as born March 1811 against 1812.
5. **FamilySearch checked for the Metz certificates.** Record pages are public, the search is not. The check nevertheless settled the question by another route: the Protestant collection cannot hold baptisms of 1702–1710 because Metz Protestant worship ended in 1685, so the four certificates are Catholic or civil, made while the family lived at Metz from about 1699 — and the eldest child's absence from them is a fact about residence, not about the child. Open lead 4 was rewritten to point at the Archives municipales de Metz instead of the wrong collection.
6. **Metz municipal archives checked.** The catalogue at archives.metz.fr was browsed directly. It confirms from the archive's own classification that the Protestant registers end in 1685, fixes the Catholic series at 1566–1793 across fifteen parishes, and establishes that no baptism index exists before 1730 — the ancien régime tables are for marriages alone. Open lead 4 now records the exact path, the two cheaper approaches worth trying first, and the fact that the four certificates cannot be found from here without a parish name.
7. **GG224 opened and read.** The Metz marriage table is browsable and legible in the archive's own viewer. Its arrangement was worked out — parish section, then alphabetical by groom — and the Sainte Croix M pages were read: MICHAUD, seven MICHELs from 1655 to 1695, then MO-, with no MICHELET. One parish of fifteen is therefore cleared. No Michelet marriage has been found at Metz, and none of this bears on the four baptisms, which remain unindexed.
8. **The Metz table read in full.** The 1893 book prints the Berlin table itself — *La Famille Michelet de Metz, d'après des documents authentiques* — numbered through eight generations, which earlier phases had cited only at second hand. It supplies the spouses that were missing: Suzanne Wiriot and, before her, **Suzanne Joly**, a first wife of Jacquemin not previously recorded here; Marie Colin with her father **Daniel Collin, praticien du palais**; Anne Philpin; and Suzanne Mangeot, the diarist. It also names **Josué Pillon** as Paul's father-in-law, dates the **Norwegian emigration of 1644** to Paul's two sons, and settles the Du Bois confusion by entering Anne *veuve Dubois* as Jacques's daughter. Eight people were added and four existing entries rewritten. The same reading exposed the chronological impossibility of the table's first generation.
9. **The table scored against the registers.** Every claim the Berlin table makes that this project had already checked against the Metz Reformed registers holds to the exact day — nine entries between 1576 and 1685, plus Anne Pilon as Paul's wife. One conflict surfaced, a Jacques baptised 1657 in the register against 1661 in the table. The Protestant registers were then located, mapped as GG236–GG253, and opened: chronological, navigable by date, and written in a hand that defeats screen reading. The conclusion is that **the table is a reliable source for the Metz generations**, so the weakness in this line is not the Metz descent at all but two specific places — the impossible first generation above Jacquemin, and the unevidenced identification of Jean Jacques.
10. **FamilySearch searched, with Chris's account.** The gated search was the bottleneck and it is now past: 1,073 Michelet entries in the Protestant collection. Six more of the table's claims confirmed, one date corrected (Susanne baptised 14 not 18 January 1579), five spouses recovered that the table never named, a death for Paul in 1668, and a seventh child for Jacques and Anne Philpin. Anne Pilon added to the tree with her marriage date and her father's office; Paul Michelet given his death year.
11. **The remaining searches run in Chris's Chrome.** All three proposed checks were made. No Michelet marriage at Metz before 1576 is indexed, so Suzanne Joly stands on the table alone. The missing baptisms of 1582–1603 could not be found, but a control search showed the index itself is incomplete, so they are untested rather than disproved. And Ancestry turns out not to hold the Berlin French Colony registers at all — its Berlin material is Lutheran and civil, and its Michelet results are the 1893 book coming back round. Lead 4 has to go to the Berlin church archives directly.
12. **The three Michelet wives.** Anne Philpin, Marie Colin and Suzanne Wiriot were followed into their own families. All three have fathers named on their marriage records: Pierre Philpin, Daniel Colin, and Jean Wiriot, of which the table gives only the middle one. Pierre Philpin and Jean Wiriot added to the tree; two probable Philpin sisters and a seventh Michelet child, David, recorded. Ancestry was checked against FamilySearch at Chris's suggestion and is not competitive for Metz: its Moselle coverage starts in 1793.
13. **Frederick Millet of 1820, chased on Ancestry.** Chris asked for confirmation or correction of Martin as his father. None found, but the negative is now precise: no Frederick baptism about 1820 in Ancestry's 14-million-record Pennsylvania church collection, no Martin Millot estate in Pennsylvania probate, and an 1864 administration index that names only the decedent. The link stands on the 1830 census tick mark alone. The search did turn up something better for the generation above — Martin, John and **Eva** Millot confirmed together at Pike, Berks County, on 9 June 1805, the first record putting them in Berks and the first sight of a fourth sibling.
14. **A persistent sweep of the open leads.** Four results. Nicholas Busby's will was found in Suffolk County probate, dated exactly as Savage said and naming William Nickerson — the first primary tie between the two families. Nicklauß Möloth's autograph was read off Strassburger's facsimile of the Lydia's list, proving the man real and literate and fixing the date at 9 October 1749. The Pike congregation was identified as St. Joseph's (Hill) Lutheran, which turns out to be a register already on the leads list. And a WWI draft registration for William Earl Williams was found in Schuylkill County, naming his father. Nothing was found for Marie Williams's mother, whose first-marriage record is not in any indexed collection reached.
15. **Source texts kept.** The OCR of every volume searched is now in `sources/` in the repository, with a README recording what each one settled.
16. **Strassburger vol. I read for the Lydia.** The lead had been to find the captain's list and get Nicklauß Möloth's age. It does not exist — only the oath list survives for that ship — so the age question is closed off, and with it any hope of settling father-or-grandfather from the arrival records. Three gains against that one loss: the printed transcription independently gives “Nicklaus Möloth”, with the umlaut and without the mark-cross its neighbours carry, so his literacy is attested twice; the ship's people came from Württemberg, Durlach, **Zweibrücken** and the Palatinate, which is a new and plausible origin for a Germanised French surname; and a sweep of all eight spellings across the whole volume returns one hit, making him the only man of the name to land at Philadelphia in the fifty years the work covers.
17. **Branching out on Sgt. Frederick: three new record classes tested, all negative.** Chris asked whether there were leads outside the sources this project had been working from. Three were found and run to ground the same day, 9 September 2026.
    - **The Goshenhoppen (Bally) Catholic registers, 1741–1819.** Washington Township, Berks County, adjoining Colebrookdale — the obvious place for a *French* family that would never appear in the Lutheran and Reformed registers searched so far. Transcribed across the early volumes of the *Records of the American Catholic Historical Society of Philadelphia*, which are free on archive.org. Volumes 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7 and 8 were downloaded and searched for every spelling: **no Millot, Milot, Mylot, Miloth or Möloth**. The only near-hits are the grain. This does not exhaust the series — the fifth instalment is in vol. 61 (1950), which is not online — but it removes the most promising Catholic angle.
    - **Hinke and Stoudt, *A List of German Immigrants to the American Colonies from Zweibrücken in the Palatinate, 1728–1749*.** The Lydia's people came from Zweibrücken among other places, and this list is drawn from the duchy's manumission protocols, giving each emigrant's **home village**. A full OCR is free at `rainerdoerry.de`. **No Möloth of any spelling.** The bound matters and is weak: nine Oberämter, some 246 named emigrants across twenty-two years, and only five blocks of entries for 1749 — against the thousands who actually landed at Philadelphia that year. The protocols catch only those who left legally and paid the fee.
    - **LEO-BW, the Landesarchiv Baden-Württemberg emigration database.** About 260,000 emigrants from Baden, Württemberg and Hohenzollern — the other two regions the Lydia drew from, Durlach and Württemberg. **0 results for Moloth, 0 for Millot.** Also weakly bounded: the database is heavily weighted to the nineteenth century, when the great bulk of the 1.8 million southwest German emigrants left.
18. **Nicholas Busby's will read, and the research images saved.** The three Suffolk County images were opened and transcribed: the will confirms Savage on every point, calls Abraham Busby, William Nickerson and John Grout alike “my sonne”, gives Anne Nickerson as “my Eldest daughter” and Sarah Grout as the youngest, itemises the physic and divinity libraries and the three Bibles, and is dated 25 July 1657 before Nathaniell Woodward and Wm Pearse. It also shows that what is filmed is the register's record copy, not the signed sheet. Alongside it, every page image this research has actually been read from is now saved under `assets/`, with a README giving each one's provenance — the Strassburger plates committed, the Ancestry plates held locally, and the reasoning for the difference set out there.
19. **The Longswamp trail, run to its end (10 September 2026).** Chris asked for the Berks deeds and the Longswamp registers after the 1767 warrant turned up. Both were worked and both came back negative, but for different reasons and with very different force.
    - **The Longswamp Reformed register, 1762–1810** — William J. Hinke's translation, copied 1919, retyped 1972, published free by the Genealogical Society of Pennsylvania as a 50-page PDF. Searched in full for every spelling: **nothing**. To make sure that was the register and not the search, every distinct surname beginning with M was listed out: Mertz, Miller, Müller, Moll, Mock, Mayer, Moser, Marsteller, Meckel, Metzger and the rest — nothing within reach of Millet, Millot, Milot or Möloth. The single "Mille" is Latin, from the header *Anno Mille Septengenti Sexaginta duo*. **Coverage caveat, and it matters**: the register is thin in exactly the years wanted — three date-mentions for 1766, two for 1767, two for 1768, one for 1770 — and only becomes dense from 1775 (thirty-one) and 1776 (forty-eight). So a baptism of the later 1760s could simply not have been entered. But **1777 carries thirty-five**, and Maria Milot's birth year is 1777, so her baptism would probably have shown had the family been of this congregation.
    - **What that leaves**: Longswamp was a **union church**, and only the *Reformed* book has been read. The **Lutheran** register of the same congregation is a separate volume (catalogued as *Church records, 1747–1907, Longswamp Reformed and Lutheran*) and is **not** among the free transcriptions; it needs FamilySearch's filmed images or Ancestry. Given that Sgt. Frederick's world — Hill Church, his 1782 comrades — is Lutheran, this is the more likely book of the two, and it is the one still unread.
    - **Berks County deeds: not reachable online, and this is a coverage fact rather than a negative finding.** FamilySearch's full-text corpus holds Berks *probate* from 1752 and Berks *court records* from 1803, but its Berks **deed** books run only **1794–1797** and **1866–1900**. The books that would carry Frederick's departure from the Longswamp land, 1767–1794, are simply not in the full-text index. Consistent with that, "Frederick Millet" appears in the whole of FamilySearch full text in Berks County **exactly once** — the Groom warrant of 1767. The deed has to be sought in the browsable (unindexed) films or at the Berks County Recorder of Deeds.
20. **Was Frederick a German speaker? Yes — and it reframes the searching.** Chris asked, and the evidence is one-sided. **Nicklauß Möloth signed the Lydia's list in German orthography, with an umlaut** — a French surname already Germanised before it reached Philadelphia, off a ship out of Rotterdam carrying Württembergers, Durlachers, Zweibrückers and Palatines. Every man standing next to Frederick is German: his 1780 company under Strouch — Kester, Eckert, Berninger, Haas, Alstat, Angstat, Eberhard, Engel, Geiger, Hartman, Kintz, Kroner, Kuser, Martz, Zimmerman — and his 1782 company under Keefer — Wentz, Lesher, Kooser, Gerhart, Moser, Lehman, Brizinger. His 1767 Longswamp neighbours are **Nicholas Harm** and **Jacob Weiss**. The Longswamp register read above is itself in German: *Kirchen Buch vor die Reformerte Gemein in Langen Schwam*, read here in Hinke's English translation. Longswamp, Colebrookdale and Oley are the Pennsylvania German heartland.
    - **What follows for the searching**: in German records he is *Friederich*, not *Frederick*. Both forms have now been tested properly on FamilySearch full text — **"Friederich Millet" returns two hits, both twentieth-century Seattle passenger lists; "Friedrich Millot" returns none**. Clean, and empty. (An earlier four-phrase `OR` query returned zero for all of these and was nearly recorded as a finding; the phrases return results individually, so that zero was a broken query, not evidence. Single phrases only.)
    - **The real German gap is the press, and GenealogyBank does not fill it.** Its eighteenth-century Pennsylvania holdings are the English papers — *Pennsylvania Gazette*, *Philadelphia Gazette*, *Aurora General Advertiser*. The German-language Pennsylvania press is where a Berks estate notice or sheriff's sale for this family would actually have been printed: **Der Wöchentliche Philadelphische Staatsbote** (1762–79), the **Germantauner Zeitung**, the **Neue Unpartheyische Readinger Zeitung** and the **Readinger Adler** (from 1796), the last two being Berks County's own. These are held by Readex, Accessible Archives and the German Society of Pennsylvania, not by GenealogyBank. **Untried, and the best remaining newspaper lead.**
21. **GenealogyBank, first searched for the eighteenth century (10 September 2026).** Every previous use of it in this project was 1816 or later — six items from the *Berks and Schuylkill Journal*, 1818–20, all about **John** Millot of Orwigsburg, and four *Jersey Journal* items of 1942–54 — and it had never been searched for Sgt. Frederick's own lifetime, nor for the *Millet* spelling. Three searches were run and all are negative:
    - **Frederick Millet, 1760–1800**: two hits, both Maryland (Baltimore 1800; the town of Frederick, Maryland, 1787, which is a false match on the place-name). **Pennsylvania: zero.**
    - **Millot as a surname, 1760–1800**: 457 hits, 105 of them Pennsylvania, but the set is dominated by the French historian **Millot's *Elements of General History***, advertised repeatedly by booksellers, and by St. Domingo war dispatches. No Pennsylvania individual of the name.
    - **Millot with the keyword *Berks*, 1760–1810**: six hits, five Pennsylvania. The two *Pennsylvania Gazette* items — 24 January 1776 and 21 May 1788 — were opened and read. **Both are false positives**: the highlighted matches fall on *Berks*, in a Receiver-General's notice of county tax-collection dates and in a land-sale advertisement for tracts near Reading and Tulpehocken. Neither page carries a person of the name.
    - Still unread from the earlier session: the *Pottsville Mining Register* of 11 February 1860 and the *Weekly Miners' Journal* of 25 December 1874 (open lead 23).

22. **German records searched, and a Millot family found in the Palatinate (10 September 2026).** Following the date collision above, Chris asked for German sources on the reasoning that Frederick was born in Europe. Searched on FamilySearch's indexed historical records.
    - **A method note that matters.** FamilySearch's default surname matching is far too loose here: a search on *Millot* in Germany returned 2,455 hits consisting largely of **Milatz, Milatzen, Melleit, Millenet** and **Milwert**. Adding `q.surname.exact=on` cuts it to **97**. Every German search on this family must be run exact, or the result is unreadable.
    - **Two real Millot clusters in Germany, 1700–1760.** The first is in **Berlin**: Claude Millot and Esther Collivaux, with at least six children baptised in the French/Lutheran registers there between 1700 and 1716 — a Huguenot household of the Berlin French Colony, the same institution the Michelets belonged to, though there is no reason yet to connect the families.
    - **The second is in the Palatinate, and it is the one that matters.** A search restricted to the Pfalz, 1680–1760, returns **24 Millot records**: **Landau in der Pfalz** — *Joannes Millot* and *Maria De Ville*, their son Joannes Petrus christened 20 February 1701; **Frankenthal** — Maria Catharina Millot, daughter of *Thomas Millot*, married 1706, and Dina Millot married 1710; **Bad Dürkheim** — Johann Adam Millot married 1788; **Lambrecht** — Catharina Dorothea Millot married 1828; and, undated, Franz, Adam, Joannes Baptista, Philipp and Alexander Millot. **Frankenthal and Lambrecht were Walloon and French Protestant refugee colonies**, Lambrecht's Walloons being textile workers — which sits well with a family whose Pennsylvania representative was a **tailor**. This is the first documented Millot population in one of the Lydia's four stated home regions.
    - **What was not found.** No **Friedrich** and no **Nicolaus** Millot anywhere in the Palatinate set; *Nicolaus Millot* worldwide, 1700–1760, returns **zero**. And **Möloth/Moloth**, searched exact for 1650–1780, returns only fifteen records — Bern, Austria, West Prussia, and nineteenth-century America — **none in the Palatinate**. That is itself informative: *Möloth* looks like Nicklauß's own phonetic rendering of his name into German, not an established family spelling, which is why searching for it has never worked.
    - **The bound on all of this is large and must be stated.** These are the IGI-derived extract indexes — *Germany, Births and Baptisms 1558–1898* and *Germany, Marriages 1558–1929* — which are thin and patchy for the Pfalz. Absence here proves very little. Presence is what counts, and the presence is at Landau, Frankenthal, Bad Dürkheim and Lambrecht.
    - **Next**: the Palatinate church books themselves, on **Archion** (Landeskirchliches Archiv Speyer), for those four places between about 1700 and 1750, looking for a Nicolaus Millot and for a Friedrich born about 1740–46. That is a subscription and a hand-search, not a keyword query.

23. **The Frankenthal registers reached, free, on FamilySearch (10 September 2026).** Archion is **not** needed for this parish, and the film references are worth recording exactly because they were hard to pin down.
    - **Catalogue entry**: [237970](https://www.familysearch.org/en/search/catalog/237970), *Katholische Kirche Frankenthal (BA. Frankenthal)*, "Kirchenbuch, 1686–1799", Latin, filmed 1959 **from manuscripts in the Staatsarchiv Speyer**. Covers Frankenthal, Mörsch and Lambsheim.
    - **The catalogue's own table is misleading and cost an hour.** Its Film/Digital Notes list date ranges beside image-group numbers in a way that does not survive being read as text. Constructing a `/search/film/<DGS>` URL from it lands in the wrong register: **DGS 102333045 is Frankenthal 1758–1798**, not the 1686–1742 its row appears to claim, and image 120 of it is a Lutheran page of **1791** — verified by reading the year off the image. The other number returns "film not found" by that route. **Do not build viewer URLs from the catalogue table; click the camera icon in the row instead.**
    - **The register that matters**: clicking the second row's camera icon gives **Frankenthal, Baptism Records 1729–1781**, image group **007948181**, *Item 1 of 5*, images 1–153, in Latin. This genuinely covers Sgt. Frederick's birth window. Calibration: **image 32 = 1743–44, image 35 = 1745**, so roughly one and a half images per year.
    - **Navigation note**: the `&i=` parameter on an ARK URL is ignored — it keeps the ARK's own image. Move with the on-screen previous/next arrows, or use the grid to select an image and then open its ARK.
    - **Progress so far**: images 32 and 35 read (pages 44–45 and 50–51, covering parts of 1743, 1744 and 1745). **No Millot of any spelling.** Roughly thirty images would cover 1729–1750 in full.
    - **A caveat on the whole Frankenthal line of attack**: nothing positively ties this family to Frankenthal. It is a candidate only because two Millot women married there in 1706 and 1710. **Landau in der Pfalz has an equally good claim** — the 1701 baptism of Joannes Petrus Millot, son of Joannes Millot and Maria De Ville — and its registers have not yet been checked for availability, nor has Lambrecht's. **Bad Dürkheim** is catalogue [243098](https://www.familysearch.org/search/catalog/243098), "Kirchenbuch, 1701–1829", DGS 7992042, which does cover the window.
