24. **Parish availability checked across all four Palatinate candidates (10 September 2026), and a useful discovery about Archion.** **Archion's catalogue is free to browse even without a subscription**, and it states digitisation status per volume — so it can be used to decide whether a pass is worth buying *before* buying one.
    - **Landau, Protestant (Zentralarchiv der Evangelischen Kirche der Pfalz).** The registers for our period exist and are catalogued — Taufen 1701–1788, 1718–1788, 1740–1788; Trauungen 1675–1788, 1685–1788, 1732–1788; and a mixed Taufen/Trauungen/Kommunikanten volume 1635–1788 — but **every one of them is marked "no digitized images available."** An Archion subscription would therefore buy **nothing at all** for Protestant Landau. This is the single most useful thing learned today about whether to pay.
    - **Landau, Catholic (Bistumsarchiv Speyer)** is listed on Archion, with Mörlheim, Mörzheim and Queichheim as subdivisions, but the archive index gives no per-volume dates or digitisation status. Since the one Millot household we have at Landau — Joannes Millot and Maria De Ville, 1701 — is a Latin entry and so probably Catholic, **this is the page that still needs checking**, and it is free to check.
    - On FamilySearch, Landau's own parish appears in the catalogue as *Katholische Kirche Sankt Martin (BA. Landau)*, but its filmed holdings look to be weighted to **death registers** (1785–93, 1807–45, 1846–69, 1860–83) rather than eighteenth-century baptisms. Note also that catalogue entries reading "BA. Landau" are the *Bezirksamt*, the district — catalogue 205953, for instance, is **Essingen**, a village in that district, not Landau town.
    - **Frankenthal, Bad Dürkheim and Lambrecht** are all listed in the Catholic archive on Archion as well; none has been checked volume by volume yet. Frankenthal is in hand free on FamilySearch regardless (item 23), and Bad Dürkheim is catalogue 243098 / DGS 7992042.
    - **Landau is now closed on both platforms for our period, checked 10 September 2026.** Archion's **Protestant** Landau volumes are catalogued but every one is marked *no digitized images available*. Archion's **Catholic** Landau parish is **St. Maria**, and its digitised registers begin at **1795** — baptisms 1795–1801 onward, marriages from 1795, deaths from 1801 — with nothing earlier. And FamilySearch holds exactly **one** church-records title for Landau in der Pfalz: *Kirchenbuch, 1936–1944*, of the **Militärgemeinde**. There is no eighteenth-century Landau register readable on either site.
    - That leaves a live puzzle worth stating: the 1701 baptism of Joannes Petrus Millot **is** indexed by FamilySearch, in *Germany, Births and Baptisms, 1558–1898*, so it was extracted from something. That index is IGI-derived and much of it was abstracted decades ago from films catalogued differently or since re-catalogued; the underlying source is not evident from the search results, and the record detail page would not open. **Opening that record and reading its source citation is the next concrete step for Landau**, and it is free.
    - **A larger observation from the same searches.** Restricting to exact *Millot* around 1700 returns, overwhelmingly, **Haute-Saône in Franche-Comté** — Authoison, Vesoul and neighbouring parishes — together with the Vosges and Nord. Dozens of Jean and Joannes Millots. **Haute-Saône is the heartland of this surname**, in eastern France against the Lorraine and Alsace border. A Millot family reaching the Palatinate from there is an ordinary migration, and it is the same corner of France the Michelets came out of at Metz. That does not connect the two families, but it says where a Palatine Millot most plausibly came from before the Palatinate.
    - **Bad Dürkheim: Archion has nothing before 1760, FamilySearch has 1701 onward.** Archion's Protestant Bad Dürkheim begins with a combined volume of **1760–1828**; there is nothing earlier on the site. FamilySearch catalogue [243098](https://www.familysearch.org/en/search/catalog/243098) is *Katholische Kirche Pfeffingen (Ungstein)*, filmed 1976 **from the Stadtarchiv Bad Dürkheim**, and covers Pfeffingen with **Bad Dürkheim itself**, Erpolzheim, Hardenburg, Herxheim am Berg, Kallstadt, Kleinkarlbach, Leistadt, Weisenheim am Berg and Saline. Its contents are **Heiraten 1701–1746**, **Taufen 1728–1744** and **Taufen 1740–1750**, then 1747–1765, 1766–1798 and 1798–1829. Marriages from 1701 and baptisms from 1728 is exactly the coverage this problem needs.
    - **Reached and calibrated**: image group **007992042**, *Item 1 of 4*, images 1–203 of 628. **Image 100 is December 1739 with January and February 1740** — squarely in Frederick's birth window. No Millot on that page. The village names running through it are Weisenheim, Kirchheim, Haberstich, Winkeler, Kohr, Barth, Hartmann and the like.
    - **This is now the most promising single film in the whole search**, ahead of Frankenthal: it is free, it is reachable, its marriage register starts in 1701, and it covers a whole cluster of villages around Bad Dürkheim rather than one parish.
    - **The structural point about the two platforms, now seen three times.** FamilySearch filmed the **Staatsarchiv Speyer and municipal archives** in 1959–76 and so holds the early eighteenth-century Palatinate registers; Archion hosts the **church** archives' own later digitisation, which for these parishes begins at 1760, 1795, or not at all. For this period **FamilySearch is the better source, and free**.
    - **FamilySearch's AI full-text search does not reach German parish registers, tested 10 September 2026.** A full-text search for *Millot* returns 43,011 results, of which 30,863 are Continental Europe — but the European corpus is dominated by **nineteenth-century French electoral registers** (*Listes électorales politiques*, Paris and Seine). There is no German church-book coverage to search. The eighteenth-century Palatinate registers can therefore only be read **image by image**; there is no keyword route into them.
    - **An honest note on cost.** Covering Frankenthal 1729–1750 and Bad Dürkheim 1728–1750, baptisms and marriages, is on the order of 150–200 register images. The FamilySearch viewer resists automation: the `&i=` URL parameter is ignored, the previous/next arrows often fail to register, and the grid and single-image modes fight each other, so each image costs one to two attempts. This is a poor fit for automated browsing and a good fit for a person with the viewer open, or for a paid lookup in the Pfalz. **The odds are also only moderate**: nothing positively ties this family to either parish — Frankenthal rests on two Millot marriages of 1706 and 1710 (one naming a father, Thomas Millot), Bad Dürkheim on a single Millot marriage of 1788.
    - **The 1701 Landau record opened (10 September 2026), and it does not point anywhere.** [ark:/61903/1:1:NF5Q-JPT](https://www.familysearch.org/ark:/61903/1:1:NF5Q-JPT). It gives the christening of **Joannes Petrus Millot, 20 February 1701**, father **Joannes Millot**, mother **Maria De Ville**, and an *Event Place (Original)* of **"Landau in Pfalz Stadt"** — so Landau town proper, not a village of the district. But the citation is the generic database one and **no film or batch number is exposed**, so it gives no route to an image. The hope that it would name a film neither platform surfaces by place is not realised.
    - **The Haute-Saône and Lorraine origin cannot be pursued by name, and this is worth stating plainly.** A search for **Nicolas Millot born 1715–1735** returns **754 records**, concentrated in **Meuse** and **Meurthe-et-Moselle** in Lorraine — Ligny-en-Barrois, Bovée-sur-Barboure, Burey-en-Vaux, Fains-les-Sources, Lacroix-sur-Meuse, Colombey-les-Belles, Bure — and in **Haute-Saône** in Franche-Comté, at Vitrey-sur-Mance and Jussey. Nicolas is among the commonest given names attached to the commonest regional surname, in exactly the right years. **No amount of name-matching can bridge France to Pennsylvania here**; hundreds of men fit. Only a document that states an origin — an emigration or manumission record, or a marriage entry naming a birthplace — could make the join, and the one such source tested, the Zweibrücken manumission list, holds no Möloth.
    - Worth noting for the shape of the family history rather than as evidence: **Lorraine is the same region the Michelets came out of at Metz**. Two French-surnamed families in Chris's tree, both from the Lorraine borderlands, both Germanised on their way to Pennsylvania. There is no reason to think them connected, but it is the same migration.
    - **Reading log, Bad Dürkheim / Pfeffingen film, image group 007992042 (10 September 2026).** A navigation discovery first: the on-screen arrows do not respond to clicks, but **clicking once on the image and then pressing the Right arrow key works reliably**, and several pages can be turned and captured in a single pass. That is the technique to use on this viewer.
      **Images 100–106, baptisms December 1739 – October 1740.** Names across those pages: Bergmüller, Wachstuhl, Battenberg, Conrady, Hoffmann, Hartenburg, Kuhn, Eberhard, Heidelberg, Heck, Jörgens, Chreusburger, Wettengell, Leitmayer, Rothmann, Humbert, Schlecht, Lippe, Widden, Schönfeld, Hack, Sendel, Görgens, Flohr, Brechtel, Meesel, Carlenbach, Bracenmayer, Bechtheim, Ungstein. **No Millot.**
      **Image 107: the marriage register begins** — *Liber Copulatorum Ecclesiæ Parochialis ad S. Petrum in Pfeffingen*, opening at **Anno 1701**.
      **Images 107–125, marriages 1701–1726.** Echter, Bleffier, Pellber, Brettmeyer, Schwartz, Jörgens, Bitz, Junger, Reuter, Buderich, Wieler, Hoffman, Heyl, Koch, Zeitler, Bonneth, Bordan, Krämerin, Hebert, Hass, Schindler, Adel, Mayer, Kraft, Gilles, Groth, Häberle, Frentsch, Spring, Handle, Marthelin, Kraus, Kuntz, Hackenbarg, Bühler, Loreas, Horn, Schellenberger, Harnischerin, Hunkelmann, Schwenck, Rollenbecker, Glöcklein, Lieb, Küsther, Kundermann, Wiether, Schäfer, Müller, Betz, Reuther, Klocks, Küchner, Moosch, Ditmajer, Beckrich, Kies, Kirsch, Dinckel, Gestrin, Hörsterin, Müller, Luber. **No Millot.**
      **The priest records a gap himself**: *"Ab Anno 1710 usque ad Annum 1714 omnium copulatorum nomina in Gallicis turbis perierunt"* — the marriage names for **1710–1714 were lost in the French wars**. Any marriage in those five years is simply gone.
      **Images 107–153: the whole marriage register, 1701–1743, read page by page. No Millot, Milot, Möloth or any variant appears in it.** This is the first *complete* negative on a Palatinate parish rather than an index-based one, and it is worth its weight: the register names both spouses and usually both fathers, so a Nicolaus Millot marrying anywhere in this parish cluster between 1701 and 1743 could not have been missed. The surnames are relentlessly German and local — Echter, Lacher, Heck, Reuther, Hoffmann, Schmitt, Weissenbach, Dopper, Kohl, Jörgens, Krafft, Baader, Schwartz, Mayer, Müller, Bonn, Storck, König, Kuntz, Brill, Schuler, Breun, Dietz, Keck, Wettengall, Widder, Gans, Kramer, Seebacher — with occasional Tyroleans, Bavarians and Alsatians marrying in.
      **One French family does appear there**, which makes the absence of Millot more meaningful rather than less: **Calmadray / Calmadre / Calmadree** — Franciscus, Antonius and Wendelinus — recur through the 1730s. So a French surname in this parish would have been written down recognisably, as that one was. Millot simply is not present.
      **A second gap the priest records himself**, besides the 1710–14 one: nothing else interrupts the sequence, so the coverage 1701–09 and 1715–43 is continuous.
    - **What this closes.** Bad Dürkheim and its eight neighbouring villages — Pfeffingen, Erpolzheim, Hardenburg, Herxheim am Berg, Kallstadt, Kleinkarlbach, Leistadt, Weisenheim am Berg, Ungstein — can be **struck off** as the marriage place of a Nicolaus Millot between 1701 and 1743. The baptisms of December 1739 to October 1740 are likewise clear. That is a genuine, if negative, result, and it was reached free.
      **Images 153–158: the marriage register runs on to September 1746 and ends there**, completing the catalogue's stated *Heiraten 1701–1746*. Still no Millot. The French **Callmadry** family recurs at the very end — Antonio and Josephus Martinus, January 1746 — so the register was still recording French surnames in Frederick's own decade.
      **Images 159–161: *Catalogus Baptizatorum 1740* opens**, the separate baptism sequence. Read to February 1741: Carimin, Meteg, Widder, Mayer, Schmitz, Haale, Ling, Haaberstich, Kirschin, Sebacker, Müller, Jeck, Haas, Brosmayerin, Leuther, Heck, Heller. **No Millot.**
    - **A judgement made at this point, and the reasoning for it.** The *complete* marriage negative for 1701–1746 largely subsumes the baptism search for this parish cluster: a Millot family living there and raising children would almost certainly have surfaced somewhere in forty-five unbroken years of marriages, as either party or as witnesses. Bad Dürkheim's only positive Millot evidence was a single marriage of **1788**, four decades after our window. **Frankenthal has better evidence** — two Millot marriages, 1706 and 1710, one of them naming a father, **Thomas Millot** — so the remaining effort was moved there rather than spent finishing Bad Dürkheim's baptisms.
    - **Frankenthal, image group 007948181, Item 1 of 5 (Taufen 1729–1781).** Images 35–37 read, covering **1745 and 1746** — register pages 50 to 53. Names: Epler, Marx, Dall, Süsser, Lang, Reweinckelin, Graul, Weypler, Deswald, Majer, Spattmannin, Gärtner, Nellmajer, Bieber, Welsbach, Lohr, Oslermeyerin, Michel, Brilam, Hammer, Schadermann, Wiss, Nierwein, Hijne, Tanton, Mejer, Suchmeister, Dengel, Bauerin. **No Millot.** Earlier reading covered images 32 and 35 for 1743–45, likewise clear.
    - **Practical order of work**: Frankenthal baptisms remain the live thread — images 1–34 for **1729–1743** and 38 onward for **1747–1750** — followed by its marriage items 2–5, which is where the 1706 and 1710 Millot marriages and the name **Thomas Millot** should sit. The technique is established: click the image once, then the Right arrow key, four pages to a pass.

25. **Norway: the Egersund line opened up on Digitalarkivet (10 September 2026).** Chris asked for other parts of the tree where people could be added, and for foreign archives. Lead 21 had said Digitalarkivet would settle the Egersund questions; it does.
    - **A method note.** Digitalarkivet's plain `/search/persons` **ignores query parameters** — it silently returns an unfiltered 10,000 hits, which looks like a real result set and is not. The working tool is **`/search/persons/advanced`**, whose parameters are `firstname`, `lastname`, `birth_year_from`, `birth_year_to`, `birth_place`, `domicile`, `position`, `event_year_from`, `event_year_to`, `event_date`, and — valuable for parish registers, which index both fathers at a marriage — **`related_first_name`**, `related_last_name`, `related_birth_year`. Build URLs against `/advanced` only.
    - **The 1865 census household, Egersund, 0191 ved Mosbækveien, household 03** ([pf01038195002065](https://www.digitalarkivet.no/en/census/person/pf01038195002065)):
      - **Ole Andreas Olsen**, head, married, 52, *bødker*, **born Hitterø Sogn**
      - **Inger Marie Olsen f. Thorsdatter**, wife, married, 51, **born Søgne Sogn**
      - **Otto Johan Olsen**, son, 21, cooper, b. Egersund — the direct line
      - **Olai S. Olsen**, son, 18, pottery worker, b. Egersund
      - **Tellemine Olsdatter**, daughter, 13, b. Egersund
      - **Olava Olsdatter**, daughter, 6, b. Egersund
    - **What is new here.** Both parents' **birthplaces**, which were unknown: Ole Andreas was **not** an Egersund man but came from **Hitterø** (Hidra, the island parish by Flekkefjord in Vest-Agder), born about 1813; Inger Marie came from **Søgne**, also Vest-Agder, born about 1814. And **three children not previously in the tree** — Olai S., Tellemine and Olava — with the baptism index showing Ole Andreas as father in **1841, 1843, 1845, 1848, 1853 and 1860**, so the family is larger still.
    - **A trap worth recording before it causes an error.** Otto Johan's **sister** was also called **Olava Olsdatter**, born about 1859. His **wife** was **Olava Olsdatter born 1849**, the illegitimate twin of Ole Olsen and Marthe Olsdatter. Two different women of the same name in the same house and parish; they must not be merged.
    - **The marriage found, and it was in the wrong parish — which is why it had never turned up.** Ole Andreas Olsen and Inger Marie Thorsdatter married on **1 May 1838 at Søgne**, not at Egersund: *Parish register (copy) for Søgne 1821–1838, pp. 396–397, no. 6* ([pv00000009267859](https://www.digitalarkivet.no/en/view/327/pv00000009267859)). He is entered as **of Vesdal, born 1813**; she **of Hellervig**. They married in her parish and then moved up the coast to his working town. Every previous search had looked at Eigersund, where the record does not exist. **The transcribed index does not carry the two fathers' names**, so they wait on the register page image itself — which is the next step and would add two direct ancestors at a stroke.
    - **Added to the tree** (`index.html`, 10 September 2026): **Olai S. Olsen** (b. c. 1847, pottery worker), **Tellemine Olsdatter** (b. c. 1852) and **Olava Olsdatter** (b. c. 1859) as children of Ole Andreas and Inger Marie and siblings of Otto Johan, with parent and sibling edges and map positions; and both parents rewritten with their true birthplaces, the 1838 marriage, his trade, and the correction that neither was an Egersund native. The tree goes from 135 people to **138**.
    - **The register page read, and it names her father (10 September 2026).** The klokkerbok scan was reached through Digitalarkivet's IIIF endpoint and read at full resolution — image 159 of 272, pages 396–397, *Copulerede* 1838, entry 6. It gives, in full: **“Ungkarl Ole Andreas Olsen Vesdal af Hitterøe Sogn 25¼”** and **“Pige Inger Marie Thorsdatter Hellervig”**, married **1ste Mai**, with the two *forlovere* — the sureties standing for the match — recorded as **“Thor Svenumsen Hellervig og Ole Larsen Lindelien.”**
      - His stated age of **25¼** puts Ole Andreas's birth at about **February 1813**, and the entry names his home parish outright as Hitterøe.
      - **Thor Svenumsen of Hellervig is Inger Marie's father**, on grounds that are circumstantial but about as strong as such an inference gets: forlovere were ordinarily close male kin and most often the bride's father; he is of *the same farm* Inger Marie is entered from; and he bears *the given name her patronymic is built on*. Added to the tree flagged **probable**, not proved — her baptism entry at Søgne would settle it outright. His own patronymic carries the line one step further back, to a **Svenum or Sveinung**.
      - **How to reach Digitalarkivet page images**, worth recording: the viewer is canvas-based and exposes no `<img>`, but the tile requests reveal a **IIIF API** at `https://nye.digitalarkivet.no/api/media-file/image/<uuid>/<region>/<size>/0/default.jpg`. Requesting `.../full/max/0/default.jpg` returns the whole page — here 4176 × 3072 — which can then be cropped and read locally. The uuid is the one in the viewer's own URL. Images saved under `assets/sogne-1838-marriage/`.
    - **Thor Sveinumsen found alive in 1865, and placed (11 September 2026).** The [1865 census for Søgne](https://www.digitalarkivet.no/en/census/person/pf01038180002688) has him at **Hellesvik** — the same farm, spelt as the census-taker heard it — **aged ninety**, a widower, a *føderådsmann*, in the household of his son **Ole Thorsen**, 43, farmer and freeholder, with Ole's wife Anne P. Jansdatter and their children Gurine, Anne K., Theodor and Johan. Two things come out of it: his birth year, **about 1775**, and his **birthplace, Holme parish** — not Søgne at all, but further up the Mandal valley. That he is entered as a father at Hellesvik in 1865, thirty-seven years after standing as forlover at Hellervig in 1838, ties the two records together and makes the identification of him as Inger Marie's father materially firmer than it was.
    - **Added**: **Ole Thorsen** (b. c. 1822, Søgne), son of Thor and so brother to Inger Marie. Thor himself rewritten with his birth year, his birthplace at Holme, his standing in 1865 and his son. The tree stands at **140**.
    - **The two baptisms are not in the index, and that is the honest position.** Searched for directly: **Inger Marie's** at Søgne about 1814, including by way of her father under `related_first_name`, returns nothing in Søgne; **Ole Andreas's** at Hitterø about February 1813 likewise. One near-miss was run down and rejected — an *Ole Andreas* baptised at Hidra on 26 December 1813 whose father is **Abraham** Olsen and mother Marie Sivertsdatter of Fiddie. That child would be Ole Andreas **Abrahamsen**; ours is an **Olsen**. Not him. The Hidra register of 1781–1832 *is* indexed and holds only that one Ole Andreas in the window, so ours was either entered under a variant, baptised elsewhere, or is simply not transcribed. **Both baptisms now need the register pages browsed rather than searched** — which the IIIF technique makes possible, at the same page-by-page cost as the Palatinate work.
    - **Thor found again at twenty-four (11 September 2026).** The **1801 census for 1020P Holum** — Holme, the parish the 1865 census names as his birthplace — has **Thor Sveeinausen, born 1777**, an unmarried servant (*tienestefolk ugivt*) at the farm **Utsland** ([pf01058338001528](https://www.digitalarkivet.no/en/census/person/pf01058338001528)). Name, parish and age all answer. Three records now bracket the man: a servant lad at Utsland in Holum in 1801, standing as forlover at his daughter's wedding at Hellervig in Søgne in 1838, and a widower of ninety on his keep at Hellesvik in 1865.
    - **Inger Marie's baptism looks to fall in a genuine records gap at Søgne, and that is worth stating rather than leaving as a failed search.** The volumes were traced out one by one through `media.digitalarkivet.no/kb/contents/<id>`: **A-1 1716–1740**, **A-2 1741–1750**, **A-3 1751–1759** for Søgne sokn; **A-4 1770–1822** but for **Greipstad** sokn only; klokkerbøker **B-1 1779–1802** (Greipstad), **B-2 1821–1838**, **B-3 1848–1864**, **B-4 1864–1883**. For **Søgne sokn itself there is nothing between 1759 and 1821**. That is why her baptism of about 1814 is neither indexed nor browsable: on the present evidence **the register does not survive**. The same gap explains the empty results for Thor's own marriage and for the farm before 1821.
    - **The one route left for her parentage** is therefore not Søgne at all but the neighbouring parishes and the civil sources: a confirmation entry, a probate (*skifte*) for Thor or his wife, or the 1865 census household of a sibling. Thor's wife — Inger Marie's mother — remains entirely unnamed.
    - **Where this leads next.** The birthplaces are the route to the generation above, which is what would add *direct* ancestors: **Ole Andreas Olsen's baptism at Hitterø about 1813**, and **Inger Marie Thorsdatter's at Søgne about 1814** — her patronymic gives her father a name already, Thor. A first pass for the Søgne baptism returned nothing in the 1812–18 window, so the spelling *Torsdatter* and the Søgne registers themselves should be tried directly; Hitterø's registers likewise.

26. **Hitterø closed, and the Moyer line opened (11 September 2026).**
    - **Ole Andreas Olsen's baptism is not in the indexed Hidra register, and both candidates are now positively excluded.** The register — *Sokndal prestegjeld, Hidra sokn 1781–1832* — is transcribed and searchable, and holds exactly two children called Ole Andreas in the relevant window. **Neither is ours.** The first, born 23 May 1811, is the son of **Rasmus Pedersen**, strandsidder of Langenæss under Weisdal, and Ane Olsdatter, and the entry is marked *Død* — the child died. The second, baptised 26 December 1813, is the son of **Abraham Olsen**, farmer and pilot of Fiddie, and Marie Sivertsdatter; that boy would be Ole Andreas *Abrahamsen*, and ours is an **Olsen**. Neither age fits either: the marriage gives him as **25¼** on 1 May 1838, which wants a birth about February 1813. So he was baptised under a variant, or in another parish, or his entry was never transcribed. The census statement "born Hitterø Sogn" stands, but the baptism does not follow from it.
    - **A grep caution learned the hard way.** The OCR of these volumes carries irregular internal spacing, so a two-word search like `peter moyer` silently misses `Peter  Moyer`. Every name search across archive.org OCR must allow flexible whitespace — `(peter|jacob|martin)[[:space:]]+(meyer|moyer|mayer)` — or it will return a false negative that looks exactly like an absence.
    - **Moyer, first results from the county histories.** Roberts, *History of Lehigh County* (1914), p. 150, lists the **first grand inquest of the new county in 1812** and includes **“Peter Moyer, Sr., farmer, South Whitehall.”** That is our Peter Meyer Sr. in a civic role, and it is the first record of him found outside the FamilySearch tree. The same volume's **militia class lists** carry a **Martin Mayer** and a **Peter Meyer**, and — suggestively, given Peter's wife — a **Jacob Kern** in the neighbouring class. That is neighbourhood evidence, not descent, and is recorded as such.
    - **What the Moyer line actually needs is Northampton County probate.** Martin Meyer Sr. died in 1807, five years before Lehigh was struck off Northampton, so his will belongs to **Northampton**. A will of his naming Peter would convert the tree's weakest direct link — currently `probable:true` on FamilySearch-tree authority alone — into a proved one, and would likely name the other sons too. Peter's own death in 1838 should have left a Lehigh estate naming his children, including Jacob's heirs. **Both are FamilySearch full-text territory, and that search was signed out when this was attempted.**

27. **Martin Meyer's will: where it is, and why the search could not reach it (11 September 2026).** Chris asked for it directly; the answer is a coverage wall rather than an absence, and the wall is worth documenting because it governs this whole line.
    - **Northampton County probate is not in FamilySearch's full-text corpus.** A search on the phrase *"Whitehall Township"* returns Lehigh County records almost exclusively, and its probate series is catalogued **1812–1920** — 1812 being the year Lehigh was struck off Northampton. **Martin Meyer died in 1807**, so his will is a *Northampton* record, and there is nothing of Northampton's in the corpus to find it in. That is why the direct search returned nothing, and it is not evidence that the will does not exist.
    - **What the phrase search did return**, across the entire corpus, was a single hit: *"…credits of **Jno Martin Mayer** late of…"*, **Lehigh estate papers of 1830**. A **John Martin Mayer** of Whitehall dying about 1830 cannot be our Martin, who was born in 1730 and would have been a hundred; but the name and place make him very likely kin — a son or nephew — and the file may well name the wider family. Worth opening.
    - **The real prize turned up sideways.** The same searches show that the **parish registers are in the corpus**: *Egypt, Whitehall Township, Lehigh — Parish Records 1734–1882*, together with *Jordan Township* and *Union Church* parish records and the Western Salisbury and Walbert cemetery and vital records, 1757–1917. **These are exactly the Egypt Reformed and Jordan Lutheran registers that open lead 19 has been asking for**, and they are now keyword-searchable rather than needing a page-by-page read. A first probe on **"Catharina Kern"** returns two hits inside that collection.
    - **So the Moyer line should now be worked through the parish registers rather than probate**: Peter Meyer Sr.'s baptism about 1752 would name Martin outright and settle the tree's weakest direct link; his marriage to Catharina Kern about 1774 would name both fathers and so reach the Kern side as well. Both are searchable now.
    - **For Martin's will itself**, the routes are outside FamilySearch full text: **Ancestry's Pennsylvania probate collection**, the **Northampton County Register of Wills** direct, or published abstracts. One such exists — Anderson, *Abstracts of public records, Northampton County, Pennsylvania (and surrounding counties)* (2001), archive.org `abstractsofpubli0000ande` — but being modern it is lending-restricted rather than open text, so it needs a borrow or a library.
